I i A , • The King's Host at Newmarket.—The Kin',' has this! 41 week been the guest of Mr. and Mrs. Leopoldß,othschild at Palace House, Newmarket, where his Majesty has been attending the July meeting. The King has fre- quently been entertained by Mr. Leopold, both at Newmarket, and in Bucks, where the Rothschild . ..4 . family have altogether six fine estates. Mr. Leopold , ' 1 Rothschild is the owner of Ascot Hall. near Leighton ,4) Buzzard; Mr. Alfred Rothschild of Halton Hall; Lord ' Rothschild has a magnificent estate at Tring, including a unique museum of natural history,and there as Waddes- ton Manor, which belonged to Baron Ferdinand Roths- child. There are also Aston Clinton belonging to Lord IDLE GOSSIP Battersea,who married a Miss Rothschild, and Mentmore 4 , Towers, where dwells Lord gosebery, who also married ~ 'a daughter of the famous Jewish house. Their Majesties' Unmarried Daughter.—Princess Victoria has not been much in London this season, as, r 4 ! like her sister the Duchess of Fife, she is no greatlover of society. Her Royal Highness has just entered upon her 37th year, and people are beginning to wonder how low, she will remain the only unmarried daughter of THE CORONER'S COMMENTS. the King and Queen. Princess Victoria, however, has her own private income of £6,000 a year, and is per- fectly free to follow thel life that'appeals to her. Her pleasures are "far from the madding crowd," and though never very robust, she is fond of outdoor exercise and ' country pursuits, free of ceremony and restraint. It is - - therefore easy to understand her preference for moor and stream, and Princess Victoria has been spending .. . some weeks at Alt-na-Guisach, whilst the King and .' Queen have been untiring in fulfilling the season's engagements. , c The Busy Royal Family.—Republicanism is not likely , 4 I 1t . • to flourish vigorously on British soil, so long as we have a King and Queen who devote themselves as actively to matters affecting the public welfare as their Majesties do upon all occasions. The times have gone by when men who had nothing else to recommend them were honoured solely bn account of their exalted sta- tion, but anyone would be blind indeed who did not recognise that our Royal Family have merits of their own which fully explain the popularity which they en- joy. A crown no doubt has great fascinations, but it has also responsibilities, and sometimes involves the wearer in exacting labours. Not even the enthusiasm of a loyal populace would reconcile some people to making a journey on Tuesday to Liverpool, taking part on Wednesday in a function at Swansea, proceeding to Rhyader for the inauguration on Thursday of a water supplylfor Birmingham, and opening a hall in London on Friday. Durinc, the same week several other good causes will have During of the presence of mem- bers of the Royal Family, who do not appear anxious to test in their own persons, the soundness of a recent declaration by Lady Frances Balfour, that "there is '..., i . i nobody in the world so bored and weary, as the person with nothing to do but seek for amusement." Society and Intellectual Reading.—The late Mr. G. denounce Watts, heeeee tthhee falling genet awariset an f seediepthyifloresmopher used , ud to intellectual interests, and the spread of gambling and drinking, which he regarded as the two evils of the greatest t mag- nitude. We hear of similar complaints on the part of writers and publishers of high class literature,for which there seems to be gradually diminishing demand, hut it is in vain to hope that a literary revival will be forth- coming from such protests. In former times it is pointed out, no gentleman's education was regarded as ...! ' -T complete unless he could write decent poetry, but in 1 these days the average man or woman in Society neither t I writes nor reads verse. They play Bridge, and if they read at all, it is literature of the cheap and trashy 4 , . i kind. 1 The Imperiousness of Wealth.—Tt is often remarked that the high born are the worst mannered, and it is I equally trus that they are the least considerate for 1 others. Evidencelof this is forthcoming every London season, when there are always numerous prosecutions of fashionable dressmakers for overworking their employees. An instance which is typical of most of these cases was in Court the other pay, when it was explained that the lady for whom the dress was being made for a certain occasion, could not find time to be '- fitted before the previous morning, the gown having to be home the same night. It was therefore impossible to finish it without keeping the workpeople far into the , i night, but it was not so much the dressmaker as the , inconsiderate customer, who was to blame. Had it been THE LOVE-LETTERS CASE. said that the dress could not be finished it would have , given dire offence perhaps, to a valuable customer and • , 1 her friends, and in such circumstances, the fashionable Defendant Still in Gaol. ' dressmakers generally take the consequences of laying themselves-open to a prosecution. _ _____ The Craze for the Decollete Costume.—lt is a puzzle to many foreigners why so many women who would , look charming in some simple evening frock, with a i collar, should spoil their appearance by flaunting them- selves in bare arms and necks. Abroad, it is not the custom to appear in a decollete dress at the theatres, . , hotels. and restaurants, so that the fashion appears a little unnatural to our visitors. It is really only the very few who appear to advantage in the extreme styles of evening dress, those who are not only well formed, 1 ‘ but whose freshness of youth and beauty puts them at 4, once above all criticism. For the stout, or scraggy, the , badly made, or brown-skinned,it is folly indeed to ape a style which, if the effect is not above reproach, is very apt to jar upon all sensitively-minded persons. • Accessories.—Care in the selection of accessories , , . should, if anything, exceed that expended upon gowns ' and hats. Many a beautiful and costly toilette has been , spoiled by such items as waistbands, collars, veils, .4, gloves, or footgear, and on the other hand many an inexpensive or middle-aged gown has been saved from dowdiness by the well-chosen, and well put on acces- sories. The collarless frock is no more, andlwith it has I i . -vanished—happily—the string of imitation pearls, or coloured glass beads. Collars are high. very high, and I closely fitting, but not stiff. Tiny silk covered bones, t t :.„ , keep these semi-transparent collars in position. In gloves we are 'remaining faithful to all the palest shades, and are wearing our correct size. To cram the , hand into a glove one or two sizes too small for it, is I i to proclaim oneself hopelessly behind the times. l' ' Regarding veils, these, when walking, are only worn f ~1 .draped round the brim of the hat—not over the face— : , 1 and are mostly of shaded gauze or chiffon. Hat Strings.—We seem to have taken up the mode . ; I of hat strings with more zeal than wisdom. The tulle

varieties, generally speaking, are a success, but the same cannot be said for the closely-fitting silken strings, which make their wearers look as though their heads were bandaged. These strings boast no bows or ends, at the side, the length of ribbon being carried tightly from one side of the hat to the other. To quite young people and elderly faces only, however, are strings really becoming. The woman past het first youth, and the middle aged individual, would do well to let this idea severely alone.

MADGE.

The case of Grimoldy v. Lundy came before the Vice-Chancellor (Sir Samuel Hall) in the Chancery Court, sitting at St. George's Hall on Monday. Some weeks ago the Vice-Chancellor granted an interin injunction restraining the defendant, a Wigan man, from publishing certain love-letters written by the wife of the plaintiff. Lundy having disobeyed the injunction was committed for contempt, and is still in durance. Mr. Mather, who appeared for the plaintiff, now ap- plied for judgment in default of appearance. He said the letters were left by Mrs. Grimoldy in dgfendant's house. Defendant wrongfully took possession of them, and distributed a number among neighbours and friends of the plaintiffs and other 'persons. He continued to distribute thorn after the injunction. The application, counsel said, now was that the injunction, should be made perpetual, and that the defendant be ordered to deliver up the letters in his possession forthwith. His Honour: Where are the letters now?

Mr. Mather: I cannot say. From the affidavit in support of the motion defendant has distributed twenty eight of the letters. He has not the others with him in gaol, but where they are we do not know. The Vice-Chancellor suggested to Mr. Abbott, who appeared for Lundy, that the defendant should be com- municated with. If he gave up the letters he would be released; if not be would stay in prison. From a peti- tion sent by defendant to the Home Secretary he ap- peared to be very anxious to get out, and expressed himself exceedingly sorry for what he had done; but his sorrow must be translated into acts.

Mr. Abbott having undertaken to communicate the Vice-Chancellor's view to the defendant, the case was adjourned.

Results in Rumours of Murder at Warrington.

Baseless rumours of a murder got abroad in Warring- ton last week end and created not a little sensation. The true facts of the matter were disclosed at an inquest conducted at the Police Station on Tuesday, by Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, touching the death of Thomas Flaharty, the two-year-old son of Martin Flaharty, general labourer, lodging at 29, Rodney-street. Deceased's father stated that he last saw the child alive on Saturday morning when he went to work. Later he was told that deceased had been taken ill, and when he returned home the child had died. In answer to the Coroner, he said he had not heard anything as to the child] falling downstairs. When he arrived home on Friday for dinner he noticed that the child had a mark on his head, and was told that the boy had fallen out of bed. The boy, however, was running about. Bridget Hindes, the lodging-house keeper, said that at 6.30 on Friday morning she was in bed when she heard a noise as if someone was falling downstairs. She went up to the second flight of stairs, at the bottom of which she found the boy. He did not appear to be much the worse for his fall, and she took him back to bed. He afterwards appeared to be all right until a quarter to eight on Saturday morning. She had then taken him downstairs to dress him, when he was suddenly seized with a fit in her arms. Dr. Adams was sent for, and on his arrival he found the child dead. The boy had suffered from diarrhoea for some days, but had not been medically attended. Dr. Adams said he had madea post-mortem examina- tion. There were four small bruises on the child's back, one on the left side of the chili, and two distinct ones on theforehead. There were no bruises on the neck. In his opinion, the probable cause of death was diarrhoea, resulting in a severe convulsive attack. The appearance of the brain was consistent with death from convulsions, and there was no distinct evidence that the injuries had anything to do with the cause of death. Had he seen the child before it died, he was sure he would have certified that death was due to diarrhcea. In answer to the Coroner, Dr. Adams said there were rumours all over the neighbourhood about the case, and a great sensation was created. The Coroner: Did they think it was murder ?-- Witness: Yes, strangulation. That is why I particu- larly state that there were no marks on the neck. The Coroner: It is really death from natural causes. Sergeant Barlow (Coroner's officer) said the Chief Constable had instructed him to say that diligent in- quiries had been made throughout the neighbourhood and it was found the talk was nothing but idle gossip. The Coroner: Some of these women have nothing else to do but gossip—at least they won't do it. They are more inclined to stand at gate posts in a dirty con- dition gossiping. Dr. Adams: There were fonr or five hundred people waiting outside the house when we came out after making the post-mortem examination. The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the medical testimony.

The Examiner Saturday, july 16 1904.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAT.,

'The July sales are ever welcome and are waited for by many well dressed women who delay their visits to the country or seaside to Iremain for this annual and most notable event

in the sartorial world. There are many real bargains to be picked up at very small prices compared to what they would have been priced at six weeks earlier, and now that the English summer hardly commences till July the dainty muslins, fascinating laces, and picturesque floral hats and toques‘will not br found at all out of date. Of course every far- seeing woman will pay her visit as early as possible in the week as after the first few days the best of the bargains are gone and the rub- bish is alone left. Let me warn all my readers and especially those who are inclined to secure something because it is cheap, to sternly deny themselves anything which is made or trimmed with soiled chiffon, for this delicate material never cleans up to look as good as new as do many other dainty fabrics. Short lengths of silk or lace of any kind are useful and can be transformed into dainty blouses or

, • ak .04 •1," • t 4c, 1 11

===== 0002601_19040716_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
A PRETTY LINEN BLOUSE.

other adjuncts of dress. Indeed the v..aist hand is this season quite a feature'of dress, and there are some very charming samples made with long knotted ends which fall half way down the back of the skirt which are expen- sive when bought ready made, but can be easily made at home out of a short length of silk.

The pretty linen blouse is made with box pleats down the front in the style of a Norfolk blouse, and when finished by a neat turn-over collar and silk stock looks very smart. The lincn in this case .is pale string coloured and the embroidery is done in brown. A bright brown leather belt is worn round the waist. and the silk stock is of coffee coloured -silk spotted with brown. Worn with a linen skirt to match of short walking length together witb a rustic brown straw French sailor hat and brown leather shoes nothing could be smarter for seaside or country wear. It is quite re- markable what a favourite colour brown is this season, and we see it on all sides in a variety of shades. One very smart woman has selected all her summer gowns and hats in various combinations and contrasts of different toaes of brown and pink and has all her belts,

A SHADY HAT.

cravats, sunshades, and shoes of the same shades. The result is not only charming, but enables her to vary her dress in a wonderful manner by varying the hats, etc., with the different costumes.

The dainty hat sketched offers prospects of protection for the complexion of the wearer thereof from the tan or freckles which so often result from exposure to the summer sun. It is of fine white chip and is trimmed with an old yellowish lace scarf twisted round the crown and a cluster of white roses veiled in white chiffon, a strand of which is brought round the right side and carelessly knotted in front. The brim is depressed in front, and at the back, as is the case with so many of the prettiest and most becoming hats this season. The lace veil worn in the style of the earls' Victorian era is not one suited to every face, and indeed should not be casually adopted as it must harmonise with the scheme of the whole toilette, or it otherwise looks out of taste. I have lately seen some terrible in- stances of how ugly a pretty fashion like this can be made to appear when attempted by a certain style of woman who, however difficult a mode may be to achieve successfully, will attempt to exploit it and thereby renders herself as an awful example and signs the death warrant of the mode.

(c)pyright.) THE DANCER'S WILL

By JEAN MIDDLEMASS,

Author of "The Yellow Badge," "A Life's Sur- render," "In Storm and Strife," "The Case of David Lisle," "The Finding of Elspeth." "Maisie's Triumph," &c.

She was a quiet little unassuming woman, always dressed in black, yet the gossip she unwittingly created was excessive. She lived in a pretty thatched cottage, which had been built in the middle of a garden, where flowers and shrubs luxuriantly blossomed. A woman of about fifty, she seemed to have no friends, and thought of nothing but her garden. Of the gossip that abounded in Freshfield, on the outskirts of which town "her little principality," as she called it, lay, she knew nothing. To the repeated questions: "Who is she ?" "Where does she come from ? " no answer was ever given. The only occupant of the cottage besides herself was an elderly woman servant, and she never gratified any curiosity. A man had been engaged to work in the garden and do any odd jobs in the house that might be required; but he was of local birth, and knew as little about Madame Rigaud as did the rest of the inhabitants. People said it was not safe for her to live in such a lonely spot with no man to protect the property at night; but Coombe would answer: "Lord love ye! there ain't nothing to protect: a few chairs and tables, hardly even a carpet; it's simple as a poor man's place; there ain't as many pots in the kitchen as I've gotten to hame. Madame's wealth is her flowers."

And he was right; they were glorious. The soil was fertile, the atmosphere genial, and Madame Rigaud's ceaseless care and cleverness in tendine, them, produced a garden of beauty of which the greatest expert .in floriculture might have been proud; in fact, but. few of them could have arranged the colours and massed them in groups as Madame Rigaud did. Yet the society of Freshfleld would not call. For some occult reason, best known to themselves, they had elected to boycott the owner of the cottage. If they had known how small was the impression their absence produced, they might have felt aggrieved; as it was, they suffered the most by their stand-offishness, since they were pining for news as to what she thought and said, about the solitary life to which they had condemned her. The only person who could give any information was the doctor. He had been called in to see old Martha, who was ill, and since that, being himself somewhat of a horticulturist, he had called occasionally to have a chat with madame about the garden. Dr. Adams was the sole link between Madame Rigaud and the Freshfleld world; yet, though she had been two years at the cottage, he knew no more than did the others from whence she had come, and, when questioned, he only answered:

"She is a most charming personality, speaks English perfectly, though with a slight foreign accent, and as for loneliness, I do not think she knows the meaning of the word." This was irritating information, to say the least of it, especially as having left it so long no one now dared to call and see for themselves this wonderful little woman, with her wonderful garden, which she seemed to be cultivating after the pattern of Paradise. Very seldom did Madame Rigaud leave her "principality" and shew herself in Freshfield. Martha or Coombe did the shoppin,g ; and, since nothing called her out of her domain, she preferred to remain within it.

A document, however, had to be signed by a Justice of the Peace—a small sum of money had been left her by a relation in France, and she could not obtain it without this signature. It annoyed her, as, silent on the subject though she was, she was fully aware that the Freshfieldites were curious and had tabooed her. That this incident would excite conversation was evident; but it could not be helped, she must face it. On a warm May morning, a dainty reticule in her hand and a white umbrella over her head, dressed as only a Frenh woman can dress, she started to walk the mile and a-half that lay between her and the house of the justice who, Dr. Adams had told her, would sign her paper. He received her unctuously and with pleasure, his wife being one of the most curious of the Freshfieldites. Madame Rig,aud was, however, on her guard, and no information was gained save that a cousin, a manufacturer in France, had lerft her about five hundred pounds. The signature as affixed, and she speedily took her departure. Delighted once more to be free, she trotted along the road, dreaming of the chrysanthemum house, she intended to build with the money on a bit of waste land at the bottom of the garden. _ .

"How she would tend and roster the curled darlings,' and, who knows, she might get a big plize " Like Pierette and her pot au bit in the well- known fable, her dream produced disaster. She caught her foot on a large stone that lay cumbering in her road, and fell. • Fortunately, she was not very far from her own gate; more fortunately still a young girl who was passing, at once came to her assistance, and set her on her feet; but her ankle was sprained. "Dear, dear lady, I am so sorry," said the girl, who was Miss Meta Franklin, the daughter of the richest inhabitant of Freshfield. Her father owned an imposing-looking villa on the hill that com- manded the town, of which the Franklins were quite the leading people. It was said that they gave themselves airs. Most assuredly Meta did not, even if the rest of the family was guilty of this social crime. She was the sweetesb. of houris, with a face full of tenderness, and which was every now and again illumined by the most bewitching of smiles. In pain though Madame Rigaud was, she could not help looking into the girl's sveet face, and think- ing she had never seen such a smile in her life before.

Meta, too, soon proved that she could be helpful .well as pleasing. "Lean on me," she said. "I am strong and big —you are such a light weight. I could almost carry you home." "I don't live very far off," murmured Madame Rigaud, who had become very white and could scarcely speak for the pain she was suffering. "I know, I know—the cottage with the green gate and the lovely flowers. How I have longed to get inside that gate, and see you and the flowers Pity, though, that our acquaintance has begun so inauspiciously." "You see me at a disadvantage—come and see the flowers. I hope no accident has happened to them," said Madame Rigaud, bravely struggling to appear at her ease, though as she put lier foot to the ground she was compelled to cry aloud from sheer physical torture. "You poor little dear!" said Meta, and, before she could be prevented, she took the tiny, fragile- looking woman up in her arms and carried her to the gate, which was only about four hundred yards distant.

Once inside it, she laid her on a garden seat, and went to summon Martha, who came in hot haste, aghast at seeing a strange young lady in the house and at hearing that her mistress had met with an accident.

Between them, they speedily placed the sufferer in a comfortable chair; but nothing would induce her to go into the 11( uie. The air in the garden would revive her, she said ; nor was she pleased to hear that Coombe had been sent off to fetch Dr. Adams. _ - -

The news that an accident had happened to Madame Rigaud, which had reached him while he was visiting a patient in the town, brought him to the cottage with all speed. "Nothing serious had occurred," he said, "but the foot would require rest for some days at least —perhaps longer. She must not walk about and look after her flowers; she must get someone to tend them for her," and he glanced at Meta Frank- lin, who beamed on them with her bewitching smile that had quite won Madame Rig,aud's heart, and said "She would do everything that was possible for the flowers." -

"She must go home now," she announced, "for they would miss her at luncheon, but would come back towards evening and do the watering." Another minute and she was gone, leaving Dr. Adams and Madame Rigaud together. "Poor dear Meta—isn't she sweet ? " he said. "Poor unhappy child!" "Unhappy, with that heavenly smile ! " exclaimed Madame Rigaud, in surpise. "It is, perhaps, On account of her smile. If she were not so bewitchingly charming, she would not be so hopelessly worshipped." a love affair. Tell me about it." Madame Rigaud delighted in the sentimental, and Meta was at. once crowned with interest.

"Oh, the old 4)et or " iler people disapprove, I t hey should do so as a mystery to be, for the ping man bears as exceedingly ,oud ellaratt,r, and is of a goGd family."

"Pon-, I suppose.!" "Jmit so ; btu thm the Fraulf.ins are so rich. What can it matter if he is L(r.oell fellow ? Meta is the only cuihl ; she lutist have all their money." "I suppose they hih expectation of seeing her wretched for life. flow many parents are thus foolish."

"Exactly, mildnine ; 1 don't think they will succeed in inak:n; lier linirry any other man. Eustace Vaal; or no one. She iias--"

"Eustace Yalu! You don't mean a ta:l, dam man, who-" Madame Rieund became so feverishly excited that she could scarcely get, out t:te words. The doctor, seeing this, and wishing to keep his patient quiet, interrupted. "Yes, yes. He was on the stage, but has now gone in for journalism. He dA this on purpose to please the Franklins ; bty, hey cannot forgive that he was once on the boards. This and his impecuciosity are indeed crimes in the eyes, of steady money-,mabbers." Madame Ilin.aud's expressive face had changed several times from white to red, and vice-versii, during the doctor's short account. He was puzzled, as he thought that some mysterious connection existed between her and Eustace Vans, whial, would probab:r bring about complications thag would stir the gossiping monotony of Freshfield into excitement, even melodrama. "Sweet Meta, with th, bewitching smile, loves Eustace Vaux," Madame Rigaud murmured, as if talking more to herself than to the doctor. It was evident she was working out a problem, and Dr. Adams, being a discreet, man, left her to it, without interruption. At last she asked: "Does he ever come here ? I have never seen him."

"Occasionally, but he has been forbidden to call at Orcott ; and, you see, machine, you so seldom go out you couldn't meet him in Fresh- field, where he sometimes loages at old Sally Taylor's. You know him ? " - "I wonder if Martha has ever seen him, or if she was afraid to tell me," murmured the little Frenchwoman, thus adding fresh fuel to the doctor's curiosity.

"He 'has not been here very lately," he said; "but several times since you came to Freshfield—. in fact, he and Meta did not know each other till after you -mine." "It is a strange world,. Dr. ...i.dams: the people in this world seem very near to each other, and yet so far away." "Exactly." But an axiom was not what Dr. Adams 7wanted at that moment. He wanted to know what con- nection existed between Madame Rigaud and tie- good-looking young fellow who was an aspirant foe the hand of sweet Meta, whom the doctor loved if she had been his own daughter. Madame Rigaud did not seem in the least inclined to tell him. He :vaitad to see if she would make any revelations ; but as she con- tinued silent and grave, as though she were in deep thought, he felt compelled to ask a question or two. "Have you seen this young ran lately? Do you consider him a fitting match fc.-: sweet Miss Meta ? " A direct answer, however, -vas not forthcoming. She only murmured: "I wish I could see him—l wish I could see him without--"

"I can easily tell him to come and see you," suggested the willing doctor. "No, no; on no account. It would be quite useless. Promise me, Dr. Adams—promise me, as you have a respect for the .secrecy of your pro- fession, that you will never allude to the secret you have surprised to-day, to anyone, in any form, or by any hint ? " "But, my dear lady, I have surprised no secret. I know nothing except that you are acquainted with this Eustace Vaux." "That is it. You must tell no one—no one, do you understand, that I ever even heard of him befire. Least of all, you must never mention my name to him, though, perhaps—" She paused for a second, then she went on more excitedly than before : "You will promise this, doctor? You will say nothing? Ala me!" And she held her hand to her head, as though oppressed by a weary pain. "I will promise everything you wish, if you will only be calm," said the doctor, who was above all professional, and was beginning to think that from this excitement owing to her accident and the shock she had received to her nervous system, his patient was ili danger of becoming feverish.

"You will not say a word ? " "I will not mention the subject to anyone, not even to Meta; and if now or at any other time you care to confide in me, I will be true and loyal. Now, will that satisfy you and enable you to rest and be at peace ? " "It is well. Thank you, doctor; but rest, peace —1? ow is it possible for those who are born of the whirlwind to be calm as mere summer zephyrs ? " He smiled at her somewhat fantastic simile; but even while he was smiling he shook his head.

"You -must keep calm, dear madame. As Igo home I will order a sedetive at the chemist's. Sleep, dear lady, sleep, that is what you require." "Sleep," she repeated. "Meta will be back before I sleep. I must see Meta." The doctor promised himself that he would manage to communicate with Meta before she returned to the cottage, mid urge the utmost dis- creti on. There was evidently some dark mystery in con- nection with this charming little woman's past life, and if, at this particular juncture, the part Eustace Vaux had played in it were not handled with the greatest care, a disastrous illness for Madame Rio•aud might, perchance; be the result. Meta received her warning, though, of course, not a word was uttered about Madame Rigaud's acquaintance with Eustace -Vaux. She went to the cottage as she had • arranged, and, being a sensible as well as a loving girl, she was fully resolved to be most • careful in her treatment of the invalid. Nor did she find that precaution would be un- necessary. Madame Rigaud' s feverish condition was by no means improved since Dr. Adams had left her. The sedative he had ordered her stood untouched on the table, the food Martha had pre- pared with no little skill and care was equally untouched; and Madame Rigaud, in a highly over- wrought condition, was living on her nerves. How long they would hold out without danger was the query. Martha, usually averse to strangers, was well pleased to see the young lady; for her mistress's condition rendered her anxious and she was glad to have someone to share her anxiety.

Not that Martha could induce Aladame igaud to eat. It seemed as though the food would not pass her white, parched lips—they would do nothing but talk, talk, endlessly talk. Yet she was not delirious—the overwrought brain was quite clear and there was no fear of her betraying secrets she did not wish to tell. Her object, apparently, wv to draw Meta into confidence, and to be told ak there was to know about Enstace Vaux.

When is a girl unwilling to talk about her love affairs when sympathetically invited to do so? Poor Meta I There were not many people to whom .the dared mention Dist ace Vaux's name, and, after the first preliminary shyness was over, as in one gush, she gave the history of her love to her new friend. .How she had met him, where she had met him, wliat he had said—it was all graphically told; and with each new revelation Madame Rigaud's tem- perature rose, and the fever that seemed to rage in her very brain increased. Meta, had she known it, was scarcely acting on the doctor's warning. She was too much engrossed in her subject to notice symptoms a. much as she would otherwise have done. Had not Dr. Adams told her to amuse Madame Rigaud and keep her from thinking about herself ? "How could ahe be thinking about herself while she was listening to the account of a new acquaint- ance's lore for a man who, till now, she had not even known by name ? " Thus Miss Meta would hate reasoned if she had reasoned at all; as it, was, she proceeded to give an account of Eustace s sayings and doings with a feverishness that almost equalled Madame R4att.a's raging condition. • For the first time since they had bean in ths cottage garden, the flowers that evenisg were total forgotten. The talk of Enstace Van; his virtu* and his fortunes, went on, till Meta was at last compelled to kiss her new friend good-night, and run off home. "It will be all right, dear love; be happy," said the little woman, as she returned the kiss, making Meta, however, start, as the touch of her lips seemed to burn. "0 madame I" she cried. "And I will come again," cried Meta, enthusi- astically. "You are sweet and dear, as well as graceful and charming. " _

- "You will not come- often, dear." "Why not? I will, I will—so often that you sill tire of me." _ _

But Madame Rigaud shook her head. She had -crown much calmer, though the hectac spots still Airnt on her cheeks.

One mOre-li;;;ing—embrace as if they had been 'Ad, old friends, and Meta was gone on her way, rejoicing in the sympathy and the friend she had found.

Short lived, however, was her joy. Next morning the news was circulated all over Fresh&ld that Madame Rigaud was dead. People could scarcely believe it. She had seemed so instinct with life, so_ unlike anyone

who would die suddenly, Nevertheless, it was true. When Martha took her an early cup of tea, she found that she had passed away. Of course, Dr. Adams was summoned, and an autopsy was necessary. Rupture of a blood-vessel in the heart had been the cause of death, he told those who asked him— with a sigh. He had conceived a great partiality for the charming little Frenchwoman, and he almost regretted that he knew aught of the fatal excitement that had conduced to this issue. Martha, who was supposed to be in Madame Rigaud's confidence, was, of course, asked about her relations, but between sobs she said she knew nothing save that madame was a widpw, and that she had been known as Mademoistlle Celestine, the dancer. _ _

The revelation was startling, even to the doctor, who forthwith began to search in a bureau which stood in madame'S bedroom, hoping that he would find some clue as to who ought to be communicated with. _

..-ku-stace Vaux ! A packet to be given him when she was no more! _ _ _

As it happened, Eustace Vaux was in Freshfield, staying at Sally Taylor's lodgings for a few days. Dr. Adams had seen him that very morning. He sent for him to come at once to the cottage. He obeyed the summons with leisurely speed, wondering as he strolled along, the morning being hot, what he could possibly have to do with the demise of this Madame Rigaud, of whom he knew nothing, save that she and her doings Were a never- failing subject of Freshfield gossip. He had not yet seen Meta or heard of the accident of the previous day. Dr. Adams was waiting for him at the garden gate. The requirements of all his patients paled before the glow of his desire to know something of the relations that existed between Madame Rigaud and Eustace Vaux. _ _ _

"Why have you sent for me ? " asked the young man, in a surprised torte. "She left a packet for you." A. packet for me ? " "You knew her well ? " "I was not even acquainted with her. Knew nothing of her save from report. What can it mean? " -

; "Come in and open the packet." Eustace Vaux, by this time, was well-nigh ac curious as the doctor, and a tremor agitated his whole frame as he took the packet from Dr. Adams's hand and opened it with nerveless fingers. ; For a minute or two there was stern silence as they stood together in the pretty drawing-room, where the lovely blossoms with which the dead woman had filled it, seemed to mock at grief with their fragrance and beauty. Eustace Vaux was reading eagerly and rapidly a document the sealed packet had contained. As he read his face grew whiter, and the lines on it—they were scarcely youthful lines—hardened. "She was my mother," he said at last, in a strange, hushed voice. "Your mother!" repeated Dr. Adams, who was by no means prepared for such a startling announcement. -

"If I had only known—if I had only known, how I would have loved her! See, here is the certificate of her marriage with my father, July 18th, 1864. Celestine Rigaud and Henry Yana."

I "But you, my dear young friend, why did you never know her ? "

"Because she was a dancer. My father died when I was a few months old, and my grand- father, who wished even to ignore the marriage, took me to Jive with him, and be educated on condition she never saw me again. I was a Vaux —a poverty-stricken Vaux—and she consented to part with me because she believed I, like the rest, should inherit the pride of race. Here, Dr. Adams, read her poor dear confession.for yourself." He sat down in an armchair and covered his face with his hands. "My mother! God only knows how I have longed for a mothrr. Poor troubled darling, and to think she too was alone, and they told me she was dead." Again there was silence while Dr. Adams read the astonishing narrative. When he had finished it, he said: "She never married again, but retook her maiden name of Rigand. Simple, brave, loving heart." "I bequeath to my dear legitimate son, Eustace Vaux, everything that I possess, and make .him the sole executor of my will. t "Signed Celestine Vaux née Rigaud, and attested by the two servants." Such was the dancer's will, at which the two men sat blankly staring for awhile, too surprised to realise the change in his fortunes that these few hours would bring about for Enstace Vaux. It was not till some days later that the full knowledge came—not till they had laid Celestine Rigaud to rest among the flowers she had loved .go well, and which were carefully arranged by Meta's lhiring hands, that inquiries anent her affairs were made by the active-minded doctor. Eustace Vaux was so overcome that he was quite worn out. Not even when he WAS told that the money left by the well-known Mademoiselle Celestine was a considerable eini, and that he would be the lucky possessor of at least a thousand a year, did ke seem to get over the loss of the mother lie had never known. Meta alone could soothe and comfort him, as she told him over and over again all the incidents of that last day of life, when she had become acquainted with the dear little woman she mourned as though she had been an old friend. . Thee, however, cures sorrows, and changes sur- prises into accepted facts. No further objection to her mairiage was Made by Meta's family. "Wite he not a Vaux, and almost a rich man?" ...;;at his mother's blood flowed in his veins, and he had sought the stage for a living, they elected to forget. In the autumn, before the blossoms began to fade, the marriage rites were performed, and the young couple took up their residence in the cottage around which the flowers were tended as lovingly as of yore, and where, before many weeks had passed, the chrysanthemum house was built in memoriam of her who had passed out of their lives before they had learnt fully to appreciate all her worth. DEATH OF EX-PRESIDENT KRUGER.

Brussels, Thursday. Ex-President Kruger died at Clarens, Lake of Geneva, at three o'clock this morning. The cause of Mr. Kruger's death was pneumonia, complicated by heart disease. He was staying at the vViisliltaor. Dsuboon ches.nnUdapy a change for the worse set in, and to Saturday last he was attending to his affairs, reading the newspapers, and receiving his strength declined. On Monday he lost conscious- ness, and the end came at three o'clock !,his morning. During his illness he was tended by his dhughter,Mrs. Eloff, and his son-in-iaw,Mr. Eloff, his own medical man Dr. Heymann, and his secretary, Mr.BTedel. The body is being embalmed, and this afternoon it will be taken in the mortuary at Clarens. An application will be made to the British Government for authority to trans- port the remains to the Transvaal, and meanwhile they will be temporarily interred here.—Reuter.

LONDON'S ROMAN BATH.

It is not generally known that London is he possessor of a Roman Bath some 2,00 C YearS old and which is filled by a continual flow of spring water. Such, however, is the case, the bath in question being situated in Strand Lane, opposite the Church of St. //lazY-le-Strand. The lane is one of the 1110 st curipus of all the curious lanes that Ina out of the Strand. Some five yards ;Toad, it wriggles its way down the Em- bankment, between modern hotels and inediaeval dwelling-houses, with many a Painful twist and contortion. A funny little 11°ase that must be very old is built right over it, the first floor forming the roof of an archway, and looking down upon the southern end of the lane from behind a Pretty green - latticed verandah hung l'ith climbing plants. The Roman bath

leaves England snortly to pay a visit to the Queen of Roumania, who is passionately fond of .him. While at Neuwied with "Carmen Sylva," the poet-queen, they are to collaborate in a grand opera on the sub- iect of Joan of Arc, she writing the libretto and he the music. His return to this country next year will be eagerly looked forward to by all lovers of music.

Is on the left, just beyond the arch. It is. 24 ft. broad and 30 ft. long, with a vaulted rotx, and the only natural light is supplied by an .karched window, almost closed up with iron ~uars. HENLEY REGATTA.

Henley this year after an unpromising, °Pening day, when the weather, though fine, Was cloudy and the attendance poor, proved as brilliant a success as ever. The other tWo days were gloriously fine and hot even on the water: and the racing was good. though often at Henley that is onlyregarded as an adjunct to the social enjoy- mentof the river carnival. The great sporting event of the week occurred, as is often the case, not in the final heats, but on the second day, when Scholes, the Canad i an sculler, defeated F. S. Kelly, the unbeaten holder of the Diamond SCHOLES, WINNER OF Sculis, in the THE DIAMOND SCULLS. second round. It was a great

race, but a-hun- dred yards from the v.inning post Kelly tracked up from want of training and had IC) be lifted out of his boat. In the final Scholes beat Cloutte, cutting the record for the event down by six seconds: but it must be admitted that all the circumstances were Particularly favourable. In the Stewards' Cup Third Trinity, the winners of the final, tied' the record, but could not beat it. It was a great week all round for Cambridge oars- raanship. Leander, which was mainly com- Posed of Cambridge men, paddled home easily from New College in the final of the Grand Challenge Cup, which is always the greatest event of the regatta. Coins College, Cambridge, won the Thames Challenge Cup from St. John's College, Oxford: and Third Trinity annexed the Visitors' Challenge Cup and the Silver Goblets. However, First Trinity Were beaten by the Eton Boys in the Ladies' I)late after a desperate finish. This result Was very popular: and the Etons crew this Year is a very fine lot. Birmingham's new comers to Henley made a successful debut, 'winning the Wyfolds from the Thames Rowing Slub. The only thing that was missed was 'he international element. But Henley, /leverless, provided interesing racing and vrai .4* grand all-round success.

CHERTSEY ELECTION RESULT.

After a stubborn fight, the Chertsey con test has resulted in the return of Lord Bingham, the figures at the declaration of the poll reading: Lord Bingham (C.), 5,425 ; Mr. T. Sadler (L.), 4,876. Excitement ran high while the counting was in progress and every hour brought up fresh motor-cars, fresh carriages, and fresh cyclists, all bright- ened with Liberal blue and white or Con- servative orange and purple. Lord and Lady Bingham and Mr. and Mrs. Sadler were in the Town Hall, and to the delight of their respective partisans were occasionally seen at the windows. One little incident pleased the crowd greatly. Lady Bingham was seen on the balcony in smiling conversation with Mr. H. H. Longman, the defeated Liberal candi- date at the last Chertsey election. LORD BINGHAM, M.P.

Earl of Lucan, K.P., by his marriage with Lady Cecilia Catherine Gordon-Lennox, daughter of the fifth Duke of Richmond, KG., and was born on December 13th, 1860. He was educated at Harrow, received a com- mission as second lieutenant in the Rifle Brigade in January, 1881, and obtaining hiE step in following July, served with thE Bechuanaland expedition in 1884-5. Lord Bingham was an aide-de-camp to the Duke of Connaught at Aldershot, and attended HiE Royal Highnest when he represented Queer Victoria at the Tsar's Coronation at Moscow. He has been a lieutenant-colonel of the Ist City of London Volunteer Rifles since 1901. Lord Bingham married in 1896 Violet, only daughter of the late Mr. J. Spender Clay, o! Ford Manor, Lingfield, and of Mrs. Beresfori Melville.

THE TIBETAN CAMPAIGN.

After the armistice in Tibet, which no on( took too seriously, General Macdonald re- sumed hostilities last week, and now it ap- pears certain that the expedition will 13( forced to proceed all the way to Lhassa. It is now impossible to turn back; and, what. ever the cost, t heGrand Llama

will have to be reduced to sub-

mission. All along the Tibet- ans have been

relying upon the

idea that their

s+rongholds

were impreg-

nable, but gy

degrees General Ilacdonald is

showing them

their mistake.

The jong at

Gyangtse,which GENERAL MACDONALD; he took by storm, is one of the strongest forts on the way to Lhassa, standing six hundred feet above the plain, and rising sheer in many places. On it stands, or rather stood, the monastery of Poukhor Choide, which is one of the most important in the country, but it is now re- duced to ruins. The new mountain guns, which are far more powerful than those ori- ginally sent with the mission, made great play.upon the walls of the fortress and carved a great breach in one side of it, and then the order was given to charge. The ascent was a terrible affair, difficult under the most fav- ourable circurhstances, but the Fusiliers, with the Ghoorkas and the Pathans, carried it with a glorious rush, Lieutenant Grant being first through the breach, although he was knocked off his ieet by a stone. Once inside, it resolved itself into a desperate hand-to- hand conflict with the Tibetans, who fought with great ferocity, and it took some hours to clear them all out of the various nooks and crannies. It was a g,loriou‘ feat of British arms, and is worthy to take rank with Dargai. Our casualties included Lieutenant Gurdon, of the 32nd Sikhs, killed, and several officers wounded more or less severely, amongst whom was gallant Lieutenant Grant, who led the assault; but, on the whole, considering the position stormed and the dogged resist- ance, our losses were very small. Now the expedition is ixady to push on, unless the Grand Llama has learnt his lesson and con- sents to treat; but it will mean a series of hard fights before Lhassa is reacbedi

SLEEPLESS SUMMER NIGHTS.

A WOMAN'S SHATTERED HEALTH WOULD NOT LET HER SLEEP, BUT DR. WILLIAMS' PINK PILLS FOR PALE PEOPLE CURED HER.

"Ten years ago," said Mrs. Roper. o f 8, Gee's houses, Cambois, near Blyth," I had a severe attack of influenza and from then 2 till quite recently I suffered from the after-effects.. Sometimes I got better for a day or two but I soon became worse again. The neighbours all regarded me as doomed. "I saw a doctor, and took a great deal of his medi- cine, and was also treated other doctors

Mrs. Roper, whose ruined digestiou was wonderfully restored by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. (From a photograph by A. and G. Taylor.). But I got miserably thin, could take little or no food and was unable to sleep at nights. When I did force myself to take food my stomach was too weak to retain it. This went on for years, and six months ago I was so ill that I had to give up housework altogether and get a girl in to do it for me. I could not walk to the foot of the garden. Every few steps I had to stop to get my _breath."

Irs.iper's case is only too common. Ruined digestion had shattered her nerves and starved her veins of blood. We see here all the signs of anwmia, and (as her story proceeds) we shall also see that as soon as her blood-supply was built up she began to grow better. "At last," Mrs. Roper continued," I had to take to my bed. I was in despair, and never expected to rise again. While in this state ,I said to my girl: I wonder if Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People would do me any good. Some people seem to think well of them.' At any rate, I determined to try." Although Mrs. Roper apparently did not know the real cause of her trouble, she had hit upon the right remedy. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills make new blood: that is the secret of their cures. Observe how quickly the effect was noticed 'I had been in bed three weeks.then,"said Mrs. Roper. " But I am telling you the barest truth when I say that I felt a change for the better after a couple of doses of the pills. On the second day I got out of bed, and I can assure you I astonished the neighbours. The im- provement was steady and rapid. I took the pills three times a day for a month, and then I dropped the dose to a dose in the morning and at tea-time. It is over ten years since I felt so well as I do now. I have sent the girl away and do all my own work now. I attribute my really wonderful cure entirely to Dr. Williams' Pink Pills." . . _ _ _

" Yes,"-iimarked her husband, who came in at this moment, "they have done wonders for her and for me, too. Three years ago I had a bad attack of lumbago in the back. I took the pills after my wife's experience, and now I ant as right as can be." In both cases the cure was the same. The new blood did it. That is the secret of the thousands of cures effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. Whatever diseases' arise from too little blood, or blood that lacks richness and purity, they can cure, just as surely as food cures hunger. Blood is the food of the body: Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are the food of the blood. The genuine pills always bear Dr. Williams' name, and substitutes are use- less and fraudulent. Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, Holborn Viaduct, London, will send a box post free for 2s. 9d. to any address. It is by making new blood that they cure anmmia, bile, consumption, indigestion, St. Vitus' dance; it is by making pure blood that they cure rheumatism, kidney disease, eczema, and all skin troubles. They improve the appetite almost from the first dose, and they give strength to everyone, especially to women, who know why they need new blood more often than their husbands and brothers.

THE NORTH WALES SERVICE.

I We understand that the Liverpool and North Wales Steamship Company have decided to cancel their pro- posed Fleetwood service for their steamer St. Tudno between Fleetwood and North Wales, and instead there- of St. Tudno will makelrequent trips from Liverpool to North Wales. From the revised July and August sail- ing bills just published it will be seen that there will be afternoon sailings on Frida,ys, commencing 22nd inst, at 2.30 p.m., in addition to , the afternoon steamer on Saturdays, Sundays and Mondays, as already advertised. Every Saturday, commencing 16th inst., St. Tudno will leave Liverpool at 11.15 a.m. for Llandudno as the extra boat (in addition to La Marguerite at 10.45 a.m.), returning at 5.45 p.m., due in Liverpool at 8 p.m. Cheap fares are announced' for this extra sailing. On Sunday, 17th July, St. Tudno will leave Liverpool at 9.15 a.m., for trip round the Isle of Anglesey. Steamer does not call anywhere else, and is due back at 8 p.m. On the occasion of , the Royal visit to Liverpool St. Tudno will leave Liverpool at 9.30 a.m., calling at Llandudno and Bangor, thence round the Isle of Anglesey, due back about 8.15 p.m. On this trip it is expected that the St. Tudno will meet and pass close to the convoy of H.M. cruisers and the Royal yacht with the King and Queen on board on their departure from Liverpool.

WARRINGTON'S BIGGEST EXCURSION.

MESSRS. WALKER'S EMPLOYEES IN MANXLAND.

Works excursions, as they are called, have a tendency to bigness. The numbers who participate in them generally run well up into the hundreds and sometimes into the thousands. Messrs. Peter Walker and Son's Brewery excursion is one to be.included in the latter category. This annual outing of the employees is an easy first in Warrington in regard to numbers. It has not yet reached the huge dimensions of the Bass employees' excursion at Burton-on-Trent, where the trippers are counted by the number of train loads, but if one may judge by the large number who journeyed on Saturday by Messrs. Walker's excursion, the local brewing trade is in a healthy stat,e. This may or may not be a matter for con- gratulation, according to the point of view taken by the reader on the drink question. No fewer than 1400 employees and friends made the journey to Manxland in the Royal Mail steamship "Queen Victoria." The generosity of the firm has undoubt- edly much to do with the popularity of the excursion. In addition to the usual allowances of the day's wage and money to spend, tickets for each man and his wife are presented by the directors, and arrangements made for free admission to the Palace and Derby. Castle at Douglas. In this respect the brewery work- man fares much better than any other employe in the town and it is not surprising to find such a' large number taking advantage of the privilege. By a decisive majority, the workmen decided to pay another visit to Douglas and once more demonstrated their fondness for going far afield: Some seven or eight years ago a similar visit was paid to the island and the pleasant reminiscences of that visit were an element in deciding upon Douglas for this year's trip. To convey such a large party by rail and sea requires I careful forethought and elaborate arrangement, but Mr. J. Fulton Smith and his capable staff have had such a large experience in this matter that they are I able to carry out the whole affair with the, precision of 1 clockwork.

Five o'clock was the hour fixed for starting from the Central Station, and at an hour when the town is usually asleep, excursionists were to be seen hurry- ing to the station. Here the brewery band in its gorgeous uniform of green and gold was welcom- ing the arrival of the trippers with a lively tune. The approaches to the station were crowded with a surging mass of humanity and pro- gress up the steps was interrupted by an irritating examination of tickets at the platform gates. Two special trains were speedily filled and sent on their way to Liverpool but still many excursionists were left on the platform. It was decided to attach some additional carriages to an ordinary train and this soon followed in the wake of the earlier ones.

Liverpool was just awakening to the day's work,and the warm rays of the early morning sun gave promise of a pleasant sail across the Irish sea. At the landing stage, the Lucania had just arrived from New York and the diAembarkation of mails and passengers made a busy scene. But there was no time for sight-seeing. Yonder in the distance was the portly figure of the captain of the "Queen Victoria" standing on the bridge and chafing under the delay which had been occasioned in our arrival. By 6.30, however, all were aboard, and we were soon steaming up the river and examining the craft and objects of inter.est aroufid. The water was as calm as a mill-pond, and with a cloudless sky above and the heat of the sun's rays tempered by a gentle zypher, the spirits of the most indifferent sea-passenger , aboard, rose. The excursionists were bent on enjoying their brief respite from daily toil and the pleasurable conditions enabled them to do so without restraint. The band, under the conductorship of Mr. Brady, struck up several waltzes, and soon the boards of the vessel were resounding with the merry tripping of feet. By way of a change, Mr. Armstrong and his choir sang several glees in a manner which testified that musical talent is not a negligible quantity amongst the brewery employees. The vessel has now crossed the , bar, and there is hardly a ripple to disturb the vessel' steady progress. Let us take a walk round. Ithe vessel to see how the trippers are enjoying them- selves. At the drinking bars, stewards are drawing corks and serving liquors with a promptness that is ' necessary to keep pace with the demand, whilst near lby are stalls of comestibles with which many are rfortifying themselves for breakfast. Everyone seems happy and contented and on good terms with every- body else. What a different picture there would have been if a rough passage had been experienced! Hardly a single person is to be seen suffering from mal-de-mer whilst little gfoups of people are enjoying themselves according to their tastes. Here a youth is displaying his skill in mani- pulating an accordion, whilst a tiny tot dances the cake-walk in a delightful fashion. Close at hand is another young fellow gesticulating with an umbrella, and, singing "Take your umbrella with you, John." Others more quietly disposed are paying a game of "nap," or keeping a watchful eye for the first sight of the island. Thus time passes on and in the short space of three and a half hours we are in full view of Douglas with its beautiful semi- circular bay, bounded by its breakway and " head" on the left and Oncban on the extreme right. The news of the excursion has preceded us, and a large number of visitors have assembled at the pier head to give the excursionists welcome. By 10.30, the trippers had disembarked and were free to spend the day according to their bent. The staff and a number of invited guests stayed behind on bard and partook of luncheon in the first;class dining saloon. Mr. Monza occupied the place of honour, and after an excellent repast, which did credit to the catering capabilities of the Isle of Man Steam Packet Company, the usual loyal toasts and cOngratu- latory speeches were delivered. Alderman Francomb dtd full justice to the toast of "Messrs. Peter Walker and Son Ltd.," which he proposed in fitting terms,and Mr. Monro suitably acknowledged. Before the gather- ing separated a' meed of praise was given to Mr. J. I Fulton Smith for his excellent arrangements on the I proposition of Mr. Monro. With a full day before them, and ideal holiday I weather prevailing, opportunity was taken of seeing some of the sights of the island. By an arrangement with the Manx Electric Railway Company, the excur- sionists were enabled to travel at a reduced fare to Laxey. This delightful spot with its great wheel and glen was reached after a journey on the car, skirting the sea coast, with its rugged headlands and minia- ture bays, forming a wonderful combination of land- scape and seascape. Some broke the journey at Groudle, with its glen, its canyon, its waterwheel and its sea lions. The miniature railway in this favourite glen was found at a complete standstill. A. grave disaster has befallen it. A short time ago the boiler of the little puffing Billy that does the haulage, burst, and the dread consequence is that the rolling stock and all connected with the affair are at a standstill until the repairs to the boiler are completed. The day was too fine for indoor amusements, and consequently there was but a sparse attendance at Derby Castle, where an orchestra played for dancing but an open-air concert in the Palace b(-mounds was more generally patronised and appreciated. The day passed all too quickly, and shortly after six o'clock, the strains of the Brewery band on the promenade were reminders that it was time to rejoin the "Queen Victoria" at the pier-head. Some of the excursionists had availed themselves of the opportunity of procur- ing extension tickets at a nominal fee. These assembled on the pier to give the excursionists a hearty send off. At the appointed time, the gang- ways were drawn in, but it was discovered that some belated excursionists were running along the pier for the boat. The captain ivaited for their arrival, and then the vessel entered upon its journey homeward. Some trippers who arrived later at the pier-head found themselves left behind and had to find Lodgings until the fallowing day. A calm passage, with a 'gorgeous sunset which shimmered in the silvery sea, were the chief characteristics of the homeward journey, Liverpool was reached about eleven o'clock and in the first hour of Sunday morning the excursionists reached their homes well-tired out, hut with pleasant I memories of the brewery excursion to Nfauxland. J. H.

A recent note in this column dealing with the refusal of a Russian priest to christen two children with the names of Corean battlefields recalls to a correspondent a story (of respectable antiquity, be it confessed) of a young English curate's admirable behaviour under similar circumstances. A wee girl baby was brought to him to be christened. The proud mother was possessed of a very marked lisp. Accordingly, when asked by the curate what name was to be given to the child she replied,' Luthy, thir." "Lucifer !" cried the clergyman in tones of horror. "Nothing of the sort. I christen this child John."

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL,

A Strong Complaint From Moore.

This Council held its fortnightly meeting at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. S. Woodward presiding. There were also present :—Admiral J. P. Jones Parry, Messrs. G. Pilling„ T. Baxter, T. Wright, C. E. Linaker, A. G. Guest, C. E. Linaker, jun., C.Roylance, J. Spruce, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, A. Broome, J. Smith, J. H. Davies, H. Mawdsley, J. T. Collier, C. Horton, J. Fryer, W. Fletcher, J. Whitley, R. Billington, T. Antwis, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (deputy-clerk), J. Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors), andJ. Farrington (inspector of nuisances.)

===== 0002601_19040716_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
A STRONG LErIER.

Mr. Wilfred J. Bleckly, of Ivy Bank, Moore, near Warrington, wrote in regard to the smell that existed at the bottom of the garden of his house owing to an open sewer of which he had made many complaints dur- ing the past three years. Were the Council waiting for an outbreak of fever before they thought it worth while moving? If an outbreak occurred and death followed the Council should be made criminally responsible. He wrote strongly because nothing else seemed to move a body which .appeared not to study in the least the health of those it was supposed to guard. Let this be the last of many letters he had written on the subject. He asked them to begin the work—it was not such a very big job—and get it done this summer. _ _

Tile Survey-or reported that work was already in pro- gress on the short length of sewer which belonged to the Council. It would, however, be necessary to con- sult with the engineer to Cie railway company, as the company owned part of the land. Mr. Linaker suggested that the Medical Officer should report as to whether the smell was injurious to health. The Chairman said a large committee saw the place only a short time ago and they were very well satisfied with the condition o things as it then was. _ _ _

It was decided that Dr. Adams should present a report on the condition of the sewage works at Moore.

SUGGESTED IMPROVEMENTS AT WESTON..

At a meeting of Weston Parochial Committee it was resolved :—" That it boa recommendation to the Dis- trict Council that the following road improvements be made in the parish of ' Weston :—Rnneorn to Weston road be widened at the point opposite the Village Hall, and the west side of the road bechannelled from the top of Cheshyres-lane to a point opposite the south end of the quarry. The west side of the said road be channelled at a point near Mr. Griffiths' farm, and the east side be eurbed and channelled from a point opposite Mr. Griffiths' farm to the Wesleyan school. The north side of the.village green .be channelled."

The recommendations were referred to the. Highways Committee.

A.NTROBUS W A.T ER_ SUPPLY.

Mr. Gerard Eaton wrote from London with regard to End the Pole-lane En water in Antrobus. He trusted now that the Council were moving in the matter of the water supply of Pole-lane end and that not only his well but the other wells there, of which there did not seem to be many, would be tested also, so that the question of the water supply might be fully considered. The Inspector was instructed to take samples and report.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
SEWERAGE WORKS AT FRODSHAM.

The Local Government Board wrote formally sanc- tioning the borrowing by the Rural Council of the sum of £333 for works of sewerage in Belle Monte Road and Bradley-lane in the lordship of Frodsham. The Board stated that no payments should be made out of the loan to a salaried official of the Council except in the case of payments (if any) due to the Clerk for legal work in connection with the object of the loanand not within the scope of his ordinary duties under the terms of his appointment as Clerk to the Council. In such a case the bills, as taxed; might properly be paid out of the loan. _

The Clerk was instructed to advertise for tenders for the carrying out of the work. .

A QUESTION OF DAMAGE.

A lotter was read from the solicitors to Messrs. Holme and King, contractors, with reference to damage alleged to have been done to the Council's roads during the extension of the Liverpool water mains. It was suggested that the matter be referred to an independent surveyor for arbitration.

The Council agreed to this course, and the Clerk was instructed to submit a list of independent surveyors at the next meeting.

HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. Dr. Adams wrote regretting that an inquest at. War- rington would prevent his attendance at the Council meeting. He had, he reported, closed Kingsley schools for three weeks and had extended the period of closing at Norley by one week. The cases notified included diphtheria (one fatal) in Frodsham, scarlet fever in Alvanley and Stockton Heath, and measles in Kingsley.

HELSBY SEWERAGE SCHEME-A DIFFERENCE or OPINION. •

The sub-committee appointed to inquire into the carrying out of this scheme presented a report in which various small alterations were recommended. The Chairman said it seemed to him that the commit- tee met with a view to condemning the scheme to all intents and purposes, and ended by blessing it. One or two little things seemed to require attention, and these were set forth in the report. - . . _

• Mr. C. E. Linaker was sorry he did not agree with the decision of the committee. The report was all very. well so far as it went, but he was of opinion that the Helsby people would have very considerable and expen- sive trouble with their system of sewerage. The Chairman pointed out that the committee had simply to deal with the carrying out of the scheme ac- cepted by the Helsby Parochial Committee. He took it that Mr. Linaker's remarks had reference to the scheme itself, and not to the carrying out of the work. Mr. Linaker : Nothing of the sort. The Chairman said one recommendation was that another automatic flushing chamber be constructed. That was essentially an addition to the original scheme and had nothing whatever to to with the work as car- ried out by the contractor. Mr. Collier seconded the adoption of the report and said there were many difficulties mainly arising from the scheme itself. As that scheme had been accepted by the Local Government Board and the Helsby Parochial Committee, he did not think the sub-com- mittee had any alternative but to accept it. Mr. Linaker said he hoped the proof of the pudding would be in the eating, and that the H lsby Committee would have no cause to regret what had been done. Mr. Guest said he was certain there were defective points in the sewer. The Chairman said the engineer and clerk of works had declared themselves perfectly satisfied, and Mr. Diggle, the expert engineer, had practically declared himself satisfied that the work had been carried out as efficiently as the generality of such work. Were the Council prepared to embark upon furtlier expense by having the sewer opened? .5.1 r. Collier did not think it would be right to involve the district in more expense without the sanction of the Helsby Parochial Committee. Mr. Roylance said he was ...ifraid that if further investigations were made there would be revelations that would astonish the Council. The report was adopted by 3 votes to 2. THE WATERING OF STOCKTON HEATH ROADS.

Mr. Linaker reported that the Highways Committee had considered this matter, and had decided that the Clerk staould ascertais whether an arrangement could be made with the County Council for the watering of roads in and about Stockton Heath, and if so, upon what terms. With regard to the diversion of the road near the Stag Inn, Walton, the Surveyor was instructed to prepare an estimate, and it was agreed that Sir Gilbert Greenall's agents be approached with a view to a contribution.

The minutes were approved. THE NEW HOSPITAL.

On the recommendation of the Isolation Hospital sub-committee. the Clerk was instructed to obtain tenders for sewage disposal work on the hospital site.

FINANCIAL.

Mr. Wright reported that the balance in hand at the last meeting was £1,146 163. 7d. The receipts daring the fortnight amounted to £lBo9s. 10d.,and the cheques drawn that- day to £616 4s. 5d., leaving a balance of £7ll 2s.

THE DUST PROBLEM. SUCCESS OF THE WIDNES SOLUTION.'

At Widnes Town Council on Tuesday, the surveyor was instructed to continue his experiments in the use of calcium chloride, which is a waste product at Widnes, as a dust layer and binder. The surveyor presented a special report dealing with the experiments in which he stated that by watering the roads with a strong solution of calcium chloride the dust was successfully laid, and the road kept moist for at hest a fortnight. In this period it would have been in the ordinary way, necessary for the roa,is to be watered at least 30 times. No damage had been done by calcium chloride, and there were no disadvantages from its use.

The benevolent permission to write a message upon one half of the "address" side of a postcard extended by our own Post-office is now also enjoyed in France, Russia, Italy,and Luxemburg, the French Post-office deserving the credit of having been the first to intro- duce it. Rather unexpectedly one learns that in Germany this minor reform is persistently refused, although many appeals have been made to headquarters for its introduction. The reason given for this is suffi- ciently amusing, lying in the amount of space necessarily taken up by a German address. Thus whereas we in England, writing to the widow of a chimney-sweep, would address her as "Mrs. ," or in France, with a shade more ceremony, they would write, "Madame veuve X-, Ramoneur," in dermany they would say, "Hochwohlgeborene Frau ' Oberschornsteinfegerswittwe."

BEAUTY OF SKIN PURITY OF BLOOD

Ancient and Modern Ideas on These Interesting Subjects.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
UP-TO-DATE METHODS

For Purifying and Beautifying the Skin, Scalp, Hair and Hands.

Socrates called beauty a short-lived tyranny, Plato a privilege of nature, Theocritus, a delightful prejudice, Theophrastus a silent cheat, Carneades a solitary kingdom, Homer a glorious gift of nature, Ovid a favor of' the gods. Aristotle affirmed that beauty was better than all the letters of rec- ommendation in the world, and yet none of these distinguished authorities has left us even a hint of how beauty Is to be perpetuated, or the ravages of age and disease defied. Time soon blends the lily and the rose into the pallor of age, disease dots the fair face with cutaneous disfigurations and crimsons the Roman nose with unsight- ly flushes, moth, if not rust, corrupts the glory of eyes, teeth, and lips yet beautiful by defacing the complexion, and fills the sensitive soul with agony. If such be the unhappy condition of one afflicted with slight skin blemishes, what must be the feelings of those in whom torturing humours have for years run riot, covering the skin with scales and sores and chatging the blood with poisonous elements to be- come a part of the system until death? It is in the treatment of torturing, disfiguring humours and affections of the skin, scalp and blood, with loss of hair, that the Cuticura remedies have achieved their greatest success. Orig- inal in composition, scientifically com- pounded, absolutely pure, unchange- able in any climate, always ready, and agreeable to the most delicate and sen- sitive, they present to young and old the most successful curatives of mod- ern times.

THE SALE OF A WIDNES WORKS. Strange Case at St. Helens.

Mr. Stewart Brown (instrudted by Messrs. Forshaw and Hawkins) appeared at St. Helens County Court on Tuesday to answers a summons by the registrar of the- court in respect of a claim made against the bailiff of the court.

Mr. Thompson (Messrs. Wiles and Thompson, Roch- dale) appeared for Messrs. George Holt and Sons,Roch- dale, the execution creditors, who some time ago got L judgment against Messrs. Connell, Fitton, and I Limited, of the Old Rosin Works, Widnes. Under:, t ; judgment Messrs. Holt issued execution, and the baili sold everything found on the premises. The Unite Alkali Company are the landlords of the Old Rosin Works, and they claimed the proceeds of the sale in payment of their claim for rent due by Messrs. Connell and Fitton.

Mr. Stewart Brown argued the legal aspect of the matter with the judge. He argued that it really could not be an interpleader action, and he really did not. know what they had been summoned for. He did not. know what Mr. Thompson would say, but he did not think there would be sufficient from the sale to pay him. There might be enough to pay the rent, but not Messrs. Holt. - -

Mr. Thompson thought the County Act met the matter.

Judge Shand: I am afraid a mistake has been made I cannot see anything else for it. Can there be any interpleader in a matter of this kind? A consultation took place, and Mr. Stewart Brown said he thought they might settle the matter. The only question was that the bailiff had claimed two sets of possession fees. There was now only £3B in court. The sale realised £49 is.

The Judge: How can the bailiff settle the matter? Mr. Brown suggested that he should forego one set of possession fees. The Judge: It is the first time I have ever heard such a suggestion in this court. It would be far too dangerous a precedent. It ha's never been done in the fifteen years I have been in this court or the Liverpool court.

Mr. Stewart Brown; Because the bailiff has never made a mistake in the procedure before, I hope. The Judge: lam not satisfied that a mistake has been made.

The case was then settled on the following terms:. £l9 10s. to the United Alkali Company, the landlords and the balance, £lB 10s. to be paid Messrs. Holt, the execution creditor.

One of the most important items in the summer train improvements this season is the running of a non-stop express between Paddington and Plymouth both up and down each week-day by the Great Western Railway. The distance run without an intermediate stop is 246 miles-50 miles longer than any other non-stop run, and the journey is performed in 4 hrs. 25 mins. The Great Western has always been famous for express trains, and these are so popular that soubrigucts such as the "Flying Dutchman," the "Zulu," &c., have, been given to the trains by travellers and others. Mr. G. A. Sekon, the Editor of the "Railway Magazine," recogniz- ing the utility of a popular name for a famous train, is offering a money prize for the best title for the new non-stop London-Plymouth Express; and Mr. J. C. Inglis, the General Manager of the Great Western Rail- way, has consented to award the prize to the name he considers most suitable for the new train.
