The rapid strokes of CONFUSION WORSE the guillotine upon CONFOUNDED. the committee stage

of the Licensing Bill must produce a rather bewildering effect on the public mind. To say that the Bill has been adequately discussed in committee is absurd enough when we consider that the changes made in it by the Government have taken • away the breath of their own supporters—or, rather, the closure has taken away that valuable breath !just as it was beginning to be articu- late. Almost at the last moment, Mr. AKERS Douglas has introduced an entirely new principle, and, if this is not the time limit which Mr. BAL- FOUR has obstinately rejected, it bears so strong a resemblance to that proposal as greatly to dis- turb the brewing interest, and its advocates in and out of Parliament. Henceforward new licenses are to be granted for a term of seven years, and then to lapse without compensation. No such licenses will be renewed, but fresh ones may take we hoe i r suppose, pl ac e s t for h tis applicantspaying handsomely separate benefit. This scheme is Sdesigned to prevent the growth of new monopoly, and is by no means welcome to the old monopolists. They cannot easily reconcile Mr. BALFOUR.S acceptance of a time- limit of seven years for new properties with his rejection of a time limit of fourteen years for existing proprties. He argues that at the end of that term the magistrates would be just agt unwill- ing to extinguish old licenses without compensa- tion as they are now. But to the new licenses he applies a guillotine which will fall at the expira- tion of seven years without giving the magistrates any opportunity of exercising their sympathies In short, two irreconcilable principles are embodied atrtn the , but the Bill and left to fight it out . Mr. BALFOUR says, that if there is the germ of a new experiment i n licensing reform, he cannot be justly accused of y Shutting b e door i tru pwoonulfdurbteh further equally gis 1 attriz 0 f . Tahnayt project which threatens to reduce the existing Jaw to chaos.

Matters have now reachnd such a pass, indeed, that the ordinary citizens may be excused for giving up the attempt to understand this extra- ordinary hotchpotch. If an old licence may con- tinue indefinitely when it was not superfluous,why should a new license be cut off in the bloom of its beauty after seven yeats ? If the old license- holder is to get compensation on the extinction

of his licence, why is the new licence-holder to have none? If the new license-holder, when he oses one licence and gets another, is to pay PrettY tiffiy for the privilege, why is this soil Par, sment a liability from which the old an" seasoned licence-holder is to be free ?" These puzzles are enough to go on with. Nut there remains the agreeable suggestion that, as ne decent trader will put his capital into a aeven years' transaction, the new licenses will breed the least desirable class of publicans. A fresh obJec" tion is that the system will multiply tied-horisea, and perhaps these two objections answer each other. Brewers, who find it to their advantage tc: increase the number of tied houses, should also fin° it to their advantage to employ rtspectable managers if they are to apply for new licences at the end of the statutory period. This all very confusing, though the confusi°": we must admit, is riot confined to the counsels 01. the Government. The argument that the c°lll- - insurance fund, out of which the trade 15 to compensate the old licence-holders, is really the property of the public, has not contributed to the lucidity of debate. We were told that this money belongs just as much to the nation as sovereign belongs to a man who paoses it from °flti pocket to another. What this singular soar- tion means is that because the trade does not pay sufficient taxation, everything °ver and above what it does pay is tric'ail the property of the State. On this reasoning, 9° shareholder in a bre very C 3 up-Lay is oricl°1:1 to his dividends;, no shareholder in any indastr! is entitled to anything if it can be shown that hie business does not furnish a proper contributi°l3 to the revenue. The truth, of course, is that the State has no moral 3laini upon a single sixPeece more than the imposts it actually levies. If theue: are not adequate let them be increased. That t"- liquor interest does not pay its fair share of tes, tion is plain enough, and we hope it will eventuall be made to pay; but, in the meantime, do not us pretend that it is not the rightful owner of I° own insurance fund.

MESSRS. GREENALL'S NEW FIRE ENGINE.

Trial at Wilderspool Brewery.

Aressrs Greenall Whitley and Company of Wilders- -11131301 Brewery ' ery were the first of local firms to establish a I,re brigade of their own, ani on many occasions it has :ndered useful public service. The firm have now Lr',,es one step further by purchasing a more powerful 11P-to-date engine to replace the one which has in use for the past 14 years. Messrs. Merryweather, London, were entrusted with the contract, and iresterdav morning there was a trial of the new engine the Brewery yard, in the presence of Sir Gilbert and Greenall, Mr. Whitley, Mr. J. Wynne-Jones, Mr. _'llll. Roberts and a member of officials and friends. 'us flew engine, which bears the name of "Empress," ,-„°ne of Messrs. Merryweather's latest type, the ta‘),reenwich Gem "pattern, and has rurnerous advan- tSes and special features. Engines of this design have t 1,7,11 supplied to many local firms. The machinery of In '8 Pattern steam fire engine is placed vertically in the Anst convenient position behind the boiler, as well as fire door at rear. The engineer riding on the foot- s can thus light up and stoke the furnace while yelling, ensuring full steam for working the -v,„',ll2Ps on arrival at a fire, and also is able to ,zil"'sh all gauges and work the interjector as required. Dcvs boiler is of Merryweather's patent water tube ern, capable of raising steam from cold water to Ii flg pressure infrom 6to 8 minutes from time of VC"Ilg the fire, and of maintaining steam easily 'hen- the pumps are running at full speed. 11-10 e boiler and chimney are handsomely lagged with ished brass,and a full set of fittings is provided. The e of the engine is of steel, strongly bolted together Ito-%nted on steel springs wrought iron axles and high Ilia ' °" spoke wheels with Mail axles for rapid travelling,. livA" engine will pump 300 gallons par minute and de- th'r a jet 150 feet high. It is handsomely finished lenghont. 5 4.t the test the time occupied in getting up steam was iznlltlin- 15 secs. This was rather longer than anticipated liin„'"vas owing to the fact that it was in an enclosed clra-a and there was no chance of getting a bottom •sevlight. The pumping capacity,of the engine was then -jet erelY tested and proved highly satisfactory. A single t 0 th a nozzle of 15/16th was projected 210ft.,whilst t;?' half-inch jets were projected 90 feet. Various other Ilrels were equally successful, and in case of fire at the opnweq, the engine is quite capable of throwing a jet inter the highest pinnacle. Mr. H. R. Hopper super- njled the experiments on behalf of the makers, and fteer Mawley was in charge of the engine. LOCAL NEWS.

Personal. We are pleased to announce that Mr. Edward G-. P. Worsley is amongst the successful candidates at the recent final examination previous to being ad- mitted as a solicitor. Mr. Worsley is the eldest son of the late Mr. James E. Worsley of Winwick and was articled:to Messrs. Woods and Sons, solicitors of this town. We regret to state that Mrs. Birtles is lying very seriously ill at her residence in Fearnhead. On inquiry last night we learned that 'there was "no change."

Coming Events. " Why I appeared before the magistrates" will be the Rev. H. Dunnico's subject at Golborne Baptist Church to-morrow (Sunday) evening. The meetings of the Spiritualist Society to-morrow (Sunday) and on Monday will be addressed by Mrs. Brown, who will also give clairvoyance. Next Satur- day a meeting of the South-West Lancashire District Union will be held at Warrington.

The last anniversary in Warrington of the Clergy Widows and Orphans' Society and Clergy Daughters' School will be celebrated on Wednesday, the 27th inst. Full particulars appear in our advertising columns. An extraordinary general meeting of Parr's Bank Limited, will be held at the bank premises, Winwick- street, Warrington, on Thursday, the 28th inst. Manchester Ship Canal Bill. This bill will be considered in committee next Tuesday. There is no opposition. Accident to a Porter. Early on Monday morning Frederick Wilson, living at Orford Post Office, was admitted to the Infirmary, suffering from a compound fracture of one of his arms. He had met with an accident whilst following his employment as a porter at Bank Quay Station.

UNDER the CLOCK for GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. The Blantyre Bone Setter.—A Local Case. A Warrington lady waited upon Mr. Rae, the Blantyre bone-setter at Bolton last week for treat- tment. Twenty-nine years ago, we understand, she dislocated her hip, and was told at the time that she was suffering from shock. She was treated for this without beneficial result, but on Mr. Rae being visited he said her hip was dislocated, and he immediately proceeded to put it in its proper place. The opera- tion was not a painful one, and for the first time for 29 years the patient is now able to put her foot down properly. She hopes to be able to walk with freedom in a short space of time.

The George Hotel Licence. Subject to the approval of the Town Council and the Licensing Authority, the licence of the George Hotel has been sold to Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, for £2,550. The Corporation acquired the hotel for street widening purposes and it is to he pulled down shortly. We understand that at the meeting of the Finance Committee at which the mat- ter was considered, Councillor Dodds and Thornton voted for the extinction of the license.

" Nothing to Work in." At the St. Helens Police Court on Saturday, Michael Logan, of 17, Antrobus-Street, Warrington, was charged with stealing clothing from the Sherdley clolliery, belonging to John Davies, John Duffy, and Owen Francis Jones, all workmen at the colliery. It appeared that certain articles of clothing were missed from the colliery, and prisoner, who had worked there, was found wearing some of the things. He was arrested by Police-Constable Littler, and on being charged with the theft, he admitted his guilt, adding that he had nothing to work in, and he intended replacing them when he drew a full week's pay.—The Bench committed him for trial at the next Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

To-day's Fire Brigade .Tournament. This afternoon the Wilderspool Football ground is to be the venue of the annual tournament in connec- tion with the North-Western District of the National Fire Brigades' -Union. An exceptionally interesting programme has been arranged, commencing at 1.45 with the inspection of the conipeting brigades which number eighteen and include Messys. Crosfield's,(War- rington), Widnes, and Winwick. The events will include five competitions, and the closing item will be the "house on fire" exhibition,introducing life-saving drills. There will be a hydrant drill for teams of girls from Messrs. Crosfield's works, and a competi- tion between .the Liverpoll and Warrington Works Brigades of Messrs. Crosfield. The prizes will be presented by Mr. A.ll. Crosfield.

CHARGE AGAINST A TRADE UNION

TREASURER.

Withholding Funds.

"SICKNESS, DRINK AND GAMBLING."

At the Warrington Police Court, yasterdly, Frederic Buckley Mellor, Brook-street, off Howley-lane, was summoned that being the treasurer of the Warrington branch of the National Amalgamated Society of Operative House and Ship Painters and Decorators and having, in his possession £7O 19s. sd. the money cf the union, he unlawfully and wilfully withheld the same.— Mr. Steel appeared for the defendant who pleaded not guilty. Mr. Browne prosecuted and expressed surprise at defendant's plea, as he had already made a clean breast of the matter. Defendant was appointed treasurer of the Warrington branch of the Society in 1895. He (Mr. Browne) quite admitted that the branch committee had been somewhat lax, because although they had asked defendant from time to time whether he had paid money into the bank, they had never asked to see the bank book. Unfortunately when the audit was made on June 28th. it was found that there was a balance missing of £7O 19s. sd. The funds had all been subscribed by workingmen. When the matter was reported to the general secretary, the defendant along with the Rev. C. Harvey Cook, of Cairo-street Chapel, went before the committee, admitted everything and begged that the offence should be looked over. Defend- ant said he had been betting and drinking. The pro- ceedings were taken under the Trades Union Act of 1871, which said that the Bench had the power to order the payment of the money lost or missing, and also to impose a penalty not exceeding £2O and costs. The case was as bad as it could be. In January defendant was advised by Mr. Cook to stop betting, but he would not, and the result was that he had gone from bad to worse.

Mr. George M. Sunley, J.P., of Salford, general secretary of the Society, submitted rules detailing the treasurer's duties. Defendant admitted to him that he was £7O 19s. sd. short in his cash. The Rev. C. H. Cook appealed to him on behalf of defendant's wife and family, stating that he had very little sympathy for the defendant, but he had a great deal for the wife and children. A few days later defendant and the Rev. C. H. Cook went, before the committee and pleaded for leniency, defendant again admitting that he was £7O 19s. sd. short. On withess asking defendant what he had been doing, he replied that he had been drinking and gambling. Mr. Cook stated that in Janu- ary the defendant spoke to him about being short of money, and he then advised defendant to go to him (witness) and make a clean breast of the whole thing. Instead of, .doing that, defendant went from bad to worse.

In answer to Mr. Steel, witness said the treasurer's salary varied according to the number of members of the branch. The committee did not lay down a hard and fast rule as to how much money the treasurer had to keep in hand. In winter, when out-of-work benefits were paid,he must have a larger amount in hand than he had in summer.

Mr. Steel: Don't you think £47 us. nd. was a large sum to leave in his hands?

Witness: Not in the winter.

Further questioned-, witness said defendant told him that when he had to produce money to the auditors at the half-yearly audits, he obtained loans from public house landlords, and afterwards paid them back. He did not know whether the defendant had carried out the rule regulating his duties in its entirety. Witness had not gone trough the books to see whether they showed that there was a balance due from the defendant of £7O 19s. sd. The auditors had examined them

The deputy magistrates' clerk (Mr. E. A. White): You admit the balance is owing. Mr. Steel : Yes. The defence is entirely legal. Con- tinuing he contended that defendant had complied with the rules, and there could be no criminal charge. It was simply a question of debtor and creditor with the society. Further questioned, Mr. Sunley admitted that he told the defendant that if he could raise half the money the society would not take proceedings against him, and he could pay the remainder by instalments. Mr. Steel : I want to know whether there has been any falsification of accounts. Mr. Browne: Absolutely none.

Mr. Steel: And the accounts show there is £7O 19s. sd. owing? Mr. Browne: That is admitted.

_ Mr. Steel then quoted cases under the Friendly Societies Act, in which it was held that inability to pay over money to the trustees did not constitute with- holding or misapplying the funds of a society. It must be shown that the treasurer was guilty of some fraud or misrepresentation. The treasurer, contended Mr. Steel, might use the money as his own without incur- ring any liability beyond the indebtedness. In this case the books had not been falsified and showed every detail as to the balance due from the defendant. There was therefore, no criminal intent. Mr. Browne said the cases quoted by Mr. Steel were taken under a different Act of Parliament. _ _ _

The Deputy 'Magistrates' Clerk ruled that unless Mr. Steel could find something in the Trades Union Act i corresponding to section 32 n the Friendly Societies' Act the cases quoted were not authorities. Under the Friendly Societies' Act there was a general section similar to the one in the Trades Union Act and a special section providing for civil proceedings against the treasurer. Under the Trades Union Act there was onel section relating to proceedings in all cases. At the request of Mr. Steel the magistrates expressed,their , willingness to state a case. Mr. Steel said the defendant was a well known War- rington man and hitherto had held an exemplary character. It was owing to sickness of the family that he came to interfere with the funds of the society. His wife and two children were • in delicate health and had to be medically attended. Defendant was out of work for periods of sixteen weeks and thirteen weeks. He had to incur considerable expense to send one of his boys to Liverpool hospital, and he was tempted V) take some money. He then did what many another man had done and took drink inithe hope of drowning his sorrows. Instead, he (Mr. Steel) was afraid he drowned his character. Afterwards he foolishly took more money and used it in betting in the hope that he would get all back and re- pay the society. At his (Mr. Steel's) suggestion he made a clean breast of everything to the general secretary. In conclusion Mr. Steel pointed out that if the Bench ordered the defendant to at once repay the money and a fine, he would have to go to prison for three months. He appealed to them to grant time for repayment. The Mayor said the bench thought justice would be done by ordering the defendant to repay the £7O 19s. sd. within six months, or go to gaol for one month with hard labour.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN FATTIER'S CRUEL CONDUCT.

John ii;;;;;;,---do-OV-Ta;CoUre-r:--k-u-n-COrn, went home drunk on gaturday night, turned his wife out, and took his infaolt daughter from her bed. At two o'clock on Sunday morning Superintendent Ennion found him perambulating the streets. The child had only a small shirt and one skirt on, and was stiff with the cold. On mouday the Runcorn magistrates fined him in a total sum of 255. BOY'S ESCAPE FROM DROIA'NING.

Michael Downey, a lad aged fourteen, residing in Victoria-street, entering the canal at Widnes,on Monday to bathe, attempted to swim across, but as soon as he got halfway he sank. A man named Wilkinson, reached the spot with a small boat, divested himself of somc of his clothing, and lowered himself down to the bottom on the pole of a boathook. After fully a minute and a half, during which time he had become exhausted him- self, and was in the act of rising to the surface, he luckily caught hold of Downey's leg. Both were pulled out, the lad being to all appearances dead. Two men from the works rendered first aid. Artificial respira- tion was applied, and the boy btought round sufficiently to be taken home wrapped in blankets.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS BOY'S DODGE.

Robert Cunfiffe, a boy of fourteen .living at 48, Lascelles-street, went into a fruiterer's slop inbGeorge- street, St. Helens, kept by Mrs. Morse, on Wednesday, and asked for change of a shilling. Mrs. Morse gave him a shilling's worth of copper and then he ran away. This caused her to look at the shilling when she found it was only a metal token. 'She ran out and the lad was stopped by a passer-by, and handed to the police. He pleaded guilty on Thursday to stealing by means of a trick, and the magistrates fined him ss. or seven days. BATHING FATALITY NEAR ST. HELE.NS.

_ Much excitement was caused in the small village of Haydock on Wednesday night by the effort to recover the body of a young man named Heath, who was drowned whilst bathing in a large pool of water near the old Beeston Colliery. Heath was about twenty years of age, and. lived in Ashton-inrMakerfield. It appears he was bathing along with some other men and got into difficulties. His body was recovered after three hours' search. The affair is very sad on account of the fact that Heath's,father was killed at the colliery close by twelve months ago.

This is very creditable for one so young, and reminds us that two years ago, Master Williamson won the special prize for obtaining thehighest number of marks in the Liverpool district, in the Intermediate Pass Division pianoforte playing of the same college.

THE HEAT WAVE.

SUDDEN DEATH OF MR.S.B. STRINGER.

Funeral Yesterday.

The death occurred on Tuesday evening, with painful suddenness of Mr. Samuel Bennett Stringer, of 150, WilderspoOl Causeway. Mr. Stringer was proceeding by the 5.28 p.m. train from Warrington to Widnes and whilst in conversation with a well-known local gentle- man he fell forward and became unconscious in a few moments. His fellow traveller did everything he could in the emergency to bring him round, but failed. On arrival at Sankey Station, Mr. Stringer was taken out of the train and carried into a waiting room. Dr Murray was early in attendance, but after examination pronounced life to be extinct. For the past year or so, Mr. Stringer had been troubled with a weak heart and death is attributed to syncope. It is suppopecl that the excessive heat of Tuesday exhausted him `and contri- buted to his death.

The late Mr. Stringer was a well-known figure in local Wesleyan circles, where he was held in the highest resperA. He was connected with both the Warrington and Widnes circuits, and had performed the duties of a local preacher for nearly 40 years. He rarely missed a preaching engagement, and was untiring in Christian service. He was superintendent of the Stockton Heath Wesleyan Sunday school, and secretary of the Local Preachers Mutual Aid Asspciation. He was connected, with the Free Church Council and the Band of Hope. Union. A teetotaler himself, he endeavoured to inculcate teetotalism in others. Some years ago he took the very practical step of organising Saturday evening, penny concerts, first in the old Wesleyan school in Buttermarket-street, and afterwards in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. These concerts proved very successful, but wore given up when the Corporation commenced Saturday evenino.b concerts in Parr Hall. He was greatly interested in the Y.M.C.A. movement, and was connected with it from its inaugura- tion to its close in Warrington. He was several times an aspirant for municipal honours, the last time in the interests of the Non-traders.. On that occasion, namely in 1903, he opposed Mr. Willia-n John Wilson in the Latchford Ward, but WAS' defAatei, 433 votes being recorded for Mr. Wilson arid 334 for NT,.. Stringer. Mr. Stringer hated canvassing at eie..;tion times and did not canvass for a single vote.

He was formerly in business at Widnes, but retired in 1887 and came to live in Warrington. He was 62 years of age and leaves a widow, and six children. The funeral took place yesterday at Farnworth. The cortege left the deceased's late residence at noon and proceeded to Bold-street Wesleyan Church, where a short memorial service was held. The Rev. G. H. Carnson conducted and he was assisted by the Rev R. Wilkins Rees and the Rev. W. C. J. Neville. Ile coffin was borne by six local preachers, namely, Messrs. M Osborne, A. Perkins, F. Wright, J. W. Sherlock, W. Hickman, and W. Collier. The chief mourners were Mr. nd Mrs. Oswald Stringer,Mr.and Mrs S. B. Stringer (Doncaster) and Mr. and Mrs. F. Stringer) (Warringtoo), sons and daughters- in-law ; Mrs. Hughes (Widnes), Miss S. Stringer and Miss A. E. Stringer, daughters ; I Mrs Peers, (Birmingham) and Mrs. Balshaw (Earlestown), stepdaughters. Mr. Chas. Stringer (Widnes). and Mr. Joseph Stringer (Earlestown) brothers; Messrs. Joseph Stringer (Doncaster). Peter Stringer (Warrington), Charles Stringer (Lonion), G. Ra,wling (Rawtenstall), W. Gleave (Warrington), brothers-in-law; Mrs. Toft sister-in-law; Mr. Hughes (Widnes), Mr. Balshaw (Earlestown), Mr. Rees (Birmingham), and a number of nephews and nieces.

A number of friends attended the service as a tribute of respect. Stockton Heath Church and Sunday school was represented by Messrs. A. Lamplugh, S. Miss, Spenseley, John Miller, R. Miller, and S. Brereton. The circuit stewards, Mr. George Weaver and Mr. J. G. Withinshaw were present,and the Free Church Council was reprecensed by the president (Rev. John Yonge.) Amongst others present were Mr. J. W. Hayward J.P., Mr. W. W. Winstanley, Mr. Patterson, Mr. G. Bailey, Mr. Waring, Mr. Robinson, Mr. Clegg and Mr. Johnson.

The Rev. G. H. Carnson delivered a brief adire3s, in whiclihe made a touching reference to Mr. Stringer's many good qualities. They did not sorrow, he said, without hope, for they had the conviction that one of these days they would meet again never to depart. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. BAC, presided at the organ and played several pieces befitting the occasion. At the close of the service,the processionwas re-formed and proceeded by road to Farnworth. Here it was met by a number of local preachers who acted as bearers and several sympathising frieais. The Rev. G. H. Carnson performed the last rites.

A Second Death on the Cheshire Lines.

It is a painful coincidence that a second death through the excessive heat occurred on the Cheshire Lines system on Wednesday. In this case also the victim was a well-known inhabitant of Warrington. Mr. Thomas J. Jowett, of 119, Marshhouse-lane. who was employed as a wiredrawer at the works of Messrs. Rylands Bros. had been on a visit to his daughter, Mrs. Owen, at Urmston fwseveral days, and on 'Wednesday evening left the house for the station to return to War- rington, apparently in his usual health. Having booked, he took a seat on the platform and a few minutes later was seen to fall across the set. When some men who were on the station went to hrn they found him unconscious, and though artificial respiration was tried, in five minutes death took place. Yesterday an inquest was held and the medical evidence showed that death was due to heart failure brought about by the excesssve heat. A verdict to this effect was returned. Mr. Jowett, who was 55 years of age, had lived in Warrington twenty-four years. In Wesleyan circles he was particularly well-known and esteemed, having been a worshipper at Bold-street Church, and a teacher at Ellesmere-street Mission. He was a staunch Liberal,and an active member of the Order of FBresters. He leaves a widow, three sons and six daughters. The funeral will take place at the Cemetery on Sunday.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Fortune again smiled on the Warringtonians on Satur- day, and they gained a brilliant victory over South Manchester at Withington. The home team could make little headway against the smart bowling and fielding of their opponents, and when the last wicket fell only 94 had been compiled. Warrington went to work with a will and when the time limit was reached their total stood at 152 with only seven wickets down. Warrington have thus had the satisfaction of beating South Manchester twice this season, the first engagement at Arpley resulting in the home team's favour to the tune of 122 to 24. Saturday's engagement marked Warrington's eleventh victory of the season, the three additional matches having ended in draws.

The bulk of the scoring for South Manchester was done by E. W. Dann who ran up 56. The Warrington bowling was difficult to deal with, W. H. Podmore acifieving the best average. His capture was two wickets for 10 runs. Lynes secured five wickets for 47 runs—a very creditable performance—and J. W. Bibby took one for 20.

An exhilarating disnlay was given by the Warrington Captain, E. A. Connor, who was in great form. He made some very powerful hits' and placed no fewer than eight fours to his credit. His innings realized 58. J. Callon rendered very valuable service, and compiled 37 (not out) without a faulty stroke. Lynes knocked up a capital 22, and J. W. Bibby made 14 (not out).

The second eleven had a surprisingly easy task in disposing of South Manchester second. The combined efforts of the visitors produced only 50, Plinston being the destructive agent. He captured four wickets at the ridiculously small cost of nine runs. The Warrington batsmen acquitted themselves credit- ably, and compiled 172 for the loss of nine wickets. Tlaewlis (41), Duckworth (28), and E. Pierpoint (38) were the top scorers.

St. Paul's gained a creditable victory at the expense of Penketh Friends' school, the totals being 81 and 26 respectively. Therelwas no tall scoring f the best per- formance being Seed's innings for 19.

By the small margin of 6, Bold-street Wesleyan; accounted for Monks, Hall, none of the players attain- ing a higher score than 10. Chester and Robinson each placed this total to his credit.

Orford Barracks had not much difficulty in defeatinz the "Guardian" printers, and here again the scoring was low, E. Gelderd (15 not out) heading the list.

Latchford Baptists had to admit defeat at the hands of Crosfield's, who ran up a margin of 23. T. Whitfield (15) did good work with the bat, the bowling honours being shared by Dargan and Atherton. The first-name 1 captured seven wickets for 14 runs, and Atherton was responsible for two for 6.

The Wigan eleven figured on the Arpley enclosure on Thursday, and Warrington were somewhat fortunate in making a draw. Owing to the heavy work of tbe past week Lynes was a little off colour,and Warringtoa were further handicapped as E. A. Connor was unable to bowl. The visitors had the assistance of Dr. Campbell, of Huyton, and T. Ainscougb of Orraskirk, who were responsible for 170 runs for the third wicket. Going on at 200 G. Hampson did a remarkable bowling per- formance,taking seven wickets for 33 runs. The Wigan innings realized 266. Warrington in reply made 136 for six wickets, P. B. If.awtin playing delightful cricket for his 53.

Lynes was playing for Warwickshire against Surrey on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, and gave a good account of himself. In the first innings he took one. wicket for 16 runs, and in the second 2 wickets for 75 Hcwas the only bowler who captured 2 wickets in this innings. He also knocked up 26.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

Bowling was again resumed in earnest on Saturday, and once more the weather was ideal. The Works Com- petition, which has proved so popular throughout the season, has q,ow almost run its course, and there was a record attendance on the King and Queen ground to witness the semi-final. The enthusiasm of the spectators was really remarkable, and showed how great was the interest in the struggle. The defeat of Houghton's Wire Works by Cheshire Lines came as a surprise to many. The railway men thoroughly deserved thevictory if only for the energy they have all shown in practising. The green was particulurly "fast," and Houghton's did not seem to get used to it. They had not practised nearly so much as their oppon- ents, and to this,their downfall must in a great measure be attributed.

There were some interesting encounters, and towards the close George Rutter and Arthur Bellian did their best to avert the defeat of the Wire Works, by beating their men 15-7 and 15-6 respectively. John Taylor, an experienced bowler, was severely trounced by J. Holbrook. The green was altogether too fast for him, and Holbrook piled on a margin of 15-1. Sam Fletcher just managed to defeat D. Bromley after a very keen contest. Bromley had a run of 11 before Fletcher got a look in. A series of' successes then fell to Fletcher, who soon led by 14-11. Then Bromley gained a single and a double, and at the fifteenth end each player had placed 14 to his credit. Fletcher was fortunate enough to obtain the winning point.

Pearson and Knowles fulalled my expectations by defeating Rylands No. 1. The secret of their success is that all the players can be depended upon. Although when half the games had been decided Houghton's were leading by one point, the forgemen asserted their superiority and ran out winners by the handsome major- ity of 29. A surprising feature was that R. Rothwell, one of the best bowlers in the forge team, was severely beaten by Fred Miller. The last-named played an exceptionally good game,and fully deserved his success. Rothwell was quite unable to make headway, and at the thirteenth end Miller claimed the victory by 15-2.

Of course, Houghton's had high hopes when the game was just in their favour, but Tom Davies's decisive victory over James Wood, the Wire Works captain. made the ironworkers' position almost secure George Ball, the captain of the winning team, played splendidly against George Holbrook and won by 15-8. The contestants never drew level and at the tenth end the position was G-8 in Holbrook's favour. Then Ball had a run of successes which gave him a creditable victory. .. . •

There was a light game between Fred Baker and James Jennings. Pearson and Knowles's organization includes many bowlers of repute. The captain is George Ball, jun., a well-known county player, than whom there is no more enthusiastic bowler in Warrington. He figured in the Lancashire County team last season, and the feature of his play is that he is always intensely in earnest. He has been connected with the Cemetery Club for a good number of years, and his father is well-known through- out the district as a veteran champion bowler.

_ The Cheshire Lines can also boast a strong team Their captain is John Monks who is considered to be one of the best if not the best trundler on the King and Queen green. Born in theneighbourhood, he has played on the green from childhood, and can always be relied upon to give- a good display. The railway team is further strengthened by the inclmion of George Higham a skilful bowler who captains the Horse and Jockey team.

There will be another great attraction next Satur- day, in the encounter betwean Winners v. Rest. The committee will select the team to represent " the rest" at their meeting on Monday. The proceeds on this occasion also will go.to the Infirmary and Nursing Asso- ciation.

On the following Saturday, a handicap main will be commenced, and entries -must be sent in by Tuesday, July 26th, at 9 p.m. Only those who are bonafide workmen and are employed at works which have entered the Cup Competition will be allowed to compete. Men who have not been playing in the Cop Competition but are bonafide workmen, will be eligible. Any workman leaving an unaffiliated works to go to an affiliated one will not be eligible to play until he has been in the latter's employment one month from date of entry,

Cycling.

Unuer the heading "Prominent Agents" last week's issue of "The Cyclist Trade Review" contained an interesting article on the business and athletic achiev- ments of Mr. W. H. Bellian, of Warrington. The writer says "It would be hard to find a better own or more highly respected cycle and motor agent in Lancashire than Mr. W. H. Bellian, of the Royal Cambrian Cycle and Motor works, Sankey-street, War- rington, and there are few more substantially built and tastefully arranged or more conveniently situated depots in the County Palatine than his. Mr. Bellian (commonly known as "Raleigh "Bellian has been established in Warrington for fourteen years, eleven of which he was in partnership with Mr. Connah, trading under the style of Connah and Bellian. Three years ago he purchased the business outright, and has remained since then the sole proprielpor. Connected with cycling from the early days when the 'good old ordinary 'was lin vogue, he has had many and varied experiences of the sport and pastime." Dealing with Mr. Bellian's premises and equipment, the writer states that the latest extension contains accommodation for about forty large motor cars. "In local cycling circles" continues the article "Mr. Bellian is well known and exceedingly popular. He has been an active and enthu- siastic member of the large and. flourishing Warrington Cycling Club for many years, and has held office for seventeen years without a break. On the racing track Mr. Bellian was one of the best known riders in the district for many years. He was a most consistent rider and raced for sixteen years in succession, winning prizes every year." An excellent photo of Mr. Bellian and an illustration of the shop front accompanied the article.

The Warrington Cycling Club's "President's Day" was favoured with ideal weather, and all thoroughly enjoyed the outing. The genial president was delighted to welcome his visitors, and a pleasing feature was that so many made the journey by road. The event is fully reported in another column.

On Tuesday evening a run to Glazebury was under- taken but owing to the stormy outlook only a dozen members put in an appearance. After spending a pleasant evening the return was commenced, but the thunderstorm made things uncomfortable for the wheelers. Warrington was reached without mishap, though at a later hour than usual.

A bowling match with Widnes C. C. was played at Sankey onWednesday,and the Widnesians were victorious by 11 points. Scores :

MOTOR NOTES.

The practice of a motor cyclist towing a weaker brother (or sister) mounted upon a pedal machine need only be mentioned in order to be condemned. The habit is a most dangerous one, not only to the towee, but to harmless persons using the highway at the same time. Quite recently, no less than three more or less severe accidents have been reported which were primarily due, in every case, to this cause.

And even more dangerous still is the practice adopted by many cyclists of" hanging on" to fast travelling motor cars. Undoubtedly, by this means they avoid a good deal of the dust that would cover them were they to keep a yard or two distant, whilst the car makes an excellent pacemaker for members of the speedy brigade. But in changing gears, and more particularly if the change has been delayed a trifle too long, the car is prone to jerk slightly backwards, and this means a nasty fall, and probably a smash-up for both man and machine. When keeping so close it is impossible to stop in time to avoid a fall, should the pacemaker slow up slightly, although even in the event of a serious accident the driver of the vehicle cannot b 3 justly blamed in the matter.

It would be well for motorists generally if the Automobile Club would devote attention to the matter of the price charged for petrol in the various houses which are supposed to be under its special patronage, and to refuse to sanction with its presence or its bless- ing those wherein an exorbitant charge is made. Indeed, it would be better still if the principal companies deal- ing in motor spirit were to insist upon a limit price and to refuse to supply those houses that were reported as exceeding this.

Just now records and attempted records for motors are the order of the day. On the top of the last triumphant 2,000 miles road run, comes a suggestion that the proprietor of a daily paper shall organise a series of reliability runs, which shall be under the rules and guidance of the Automobile Club. It may be recol- lected that a similar effort was made by the proprietors of a motor journal last autumn, but was ultimately crushed by the autocratic A.C.G.8.1.,but that is by the way.

The scheme appears to be finding considerable favour amongsbleading motorists, one of the first to signify his -intention of entering a car being, very fitly, Mr. Moffat Ford, who is the pioneer of non-stop runs himself and created both the term and the first non-stop record as long ago as 1900. He proposes to run al2 h.p. Decau- ville in these latest tests, a car similar in its mechanism to that run unofficially in the Automobile Club trials last autumn, upon which occasion, it will be remem- bered, no adjustments or repairs of any kind were made throughout the whole of the thousand-odd miles the vehicle travelled.

It is a pity that more attention is not paid to the steering gear of many of even the more expensive cars. Too often there is far too much play allowed on the steering-wheel, and when, in addition to this, the steer- ing heads are too tight, the driver is compelled to keep up a perpetual sawing-action with his wheel in order to preserve a steady course. At a slow speed this is tiresome enough; but at anything like legal limit it is not steering, in the proper acceptance of the term; it is merely knocking at the wheels first on one side and then on the other. in order to make them bite the sur- face at all. A little trouble and time spent in making a proper adjustment would speedily put. matters right ; but attention to detail is just one of the things that a lazy or careless driver bates •to consider.

The recent sad motor-car accident near Biggleswade, which resulted in the owner, Sir William Rattigan, losing his life, and his wife and the chauffeur being badly injured, draws attention to one or two points which are very likely to be overlooked by the majority of automobilists. The evidence tendered at the inquest shows that the car had, earlier in the day, already been in a collision, and that it was against the advice of those responsible for the sale that any attempt was made to take it again upon the road until it had been thoroughly overhauled.

One of the rear wheels was undoubtedly in a weak condition. In passing it may be observed that, like the great majority of motor cars, artillery wheels were used upon this particular vehicle—not because the type is

mechanically strong, but merely because public taste seems to demand that the pattern more nearly approach- ing that used upon horse-drawn vehicles shall be used. Yet suspension wheels—"bicycle wheels," as they are familiarly called—are obviously far superior in the hiatter of successfully resisting the immense strain thrown upon them when the car is travelling, although the firms who use them may almost be counted upon the fingers of one hand. The Lancaster Company is the most prominent and is, probably, the only firm making really large and heavy cars that has persisted in adopt- ing scientifically correct pattern.

They are more frequently employed upon voiturettes although, when the reverse is the case, a great flourish of trumpets in the advertisements is required to an- nounce that artillery wheels are fitted. The flourish might well be reserved for the opposition type. The Humberette is an example of a good little voiturette,hav- ing suspension wheels.

Given a weak wheel and the brakes applied at all suddenly, the result must be apparent to anyone. The spokes will be ripped out and collapse must inevitably ensue. Even without this, sooner or later, the break-. down is bound to occur, but there is no doubt that in this, as in many other cases, the careless and almost in- discriminate use of the emergency brake, which acts directly upon the rear wheel hubs, is very apt to pre- cipitate such an accident as has been recorded so recently. As its name indicates, this brake is really only intended to be used in an emergency, and is not to be brought into action upon occasions when the speed of the engine should properly be governed by retarding the spark and throttling down.

In this connection it may be as well to mention in passing that it is far the best plan to have a proper throttle fitted, even in the case of those cars which are provided with automatic carburetters. These in them- selves are excellent if well designed; but there appears to be some considerable misconception regarding their action. They are really designed to vary the air supply in exact proportion to the speed at which the motor is running. Thus, at low speeds mixture is rich in gas, while at high velocity the air aperture is enlarged and the mixture is therefore somewhat weaker, but not so weakened as to slow the engine unless the speed of the mo tor be extreme. Therefore,the automatic carburetter enables the driver to dispense with an air lever, but its action does not supply the place of a throttle which should always be fitted, as by this means the amount of mixture—irrespective of what the quantity may be— entering the cylinder can be regulated at will.

REFUSAL TO PAY TOLLS. Greengrocers Summoned by the Corporation.

INTERESTING ARGUMENTS.

For a considerable time yesterday, the Warrington magistrates heard arguments on the refusal by a number lf greengrocers to pay tolls for hawking. As a test case John Lockett, Tilley-street, was summoned on the com- plaint of the Corporation who alleged that on June 11th he exposed for sale in Greenall-street certain:vegetables fruit, and agricultural produce (not being packed in hampers, sacks or packages) in a cart drawn by one horse ; and claimed is. 6d., the amount of the toll pay- able.—Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy Town Clerk, pro- secuted, and Mr. Quilliam,of Liverpool, defended. The magistrates on the Bench were Messrs. H. Roberts (pre- siding), A. J. Jolley, C. S. Burgess, and F. W. Monks. Mr. Wilson said the claim had arisen owing to a num, her of hawkers having refused to pay any toll at el- and although the amount involved in that particular case was only small, the question was of great import- ance to the Corporation, as to some extent the right of the Corporntion to demand toll from persons hawking agricultural produce in the streets of the borough de- pended upon the decision of the bench. Section 27 of the Warrington Improvement and Market Act of 1854 prescribed that the limits of the market for the purposes of the Act should be the borough. In a schedule to the Act it was provided that every person exposing for sale hay, straw, vegetables, fruit or agricultural produce in carts,not being packed in seperate hampers,sacks or other packages should pay toll, the amount for a one horse vehicle being is. Gd. The demand for tolls was no new thing. On the contrary tolls had been paid by persons of this class since the passing of the Act, and Inspector Place would prove that he had collected tolls for the past 38 years. Formerly there was a charge of 6d. per day for each cart, but in March of last year the Corporatton fixed a scale of 13s. payable quarterly. This appeared to have met with the approval of the hawkers in general, and they paid the first quarter's amount. On the second quarter's instalment being demanded a large number refused to pay. They stated that they had formed an association, and had been advised that the Corporation could not legally enforce payment of tolls. On June 11th the defendant Lockett was found hawking in Greenall-street. The toll was demanded hy Inspector Wallington, but the defendant refused to pay, stating that he did not intend to pay any more toll, that the Association was in communica- tion with the Town Clerk, and he was waiting to be summoned.

Mr. W. H. Wallington gave evidence, and said the defendant served customers from the hampers which were uncovered. So far as he knew, defendant sold to every person who liked to buy. He did not hear him call out the price, or invite anyone to buy. Mr.E. Place, chief market inspector, said it was when the hawkers formed an association that "the mischief began." (Laughter.) He knew it was defendant's custom to hawk all kinds of vegetables and fruit and to call out.

James Pritchard, 43, Ellesmere-street, said his xvife purchased goods from the defendant on the date in linestion. She was not a regular customer.

Mr, Wiliam argued 'he case, point by point at great length. Defendant, he contended, did not in the legal sense " expose " the goods for sale as he went round to regular customers to deliver the wares. He, however, accepted any stray orders. Dealing with the statement, that the whole of Warrington was the market, Mr. Quilliam quoted the heading of the Act under which the case was brought, which was "An Act for the improvement of the borough of Warrington and for enabling the Council to erect a covered market and for other purposes." Every street could not therefore, he submitted, be the market. The tolls could only be demanded within the defined limits of the market, and the section which prescribed that "the market should be the whole of the borough " was only to limit thq power of the Corporation. It meant that the market must be within the borough. Mr. Quilliam also called attention to the fact that the receipt for the tolls was marked "hawker's licence." He contended that if it was a hawker's licence the Corpora- tion had no right to take toll. Mr. Lockett was called and stated that the goods he sold on the date in question were all bought in the Warrington market. A toll had therefore been paid on them.

The Chairman said the magistrates were unanimously of opinion that for the purposes of the Act, Warrington was constituted a market. It seemed to them that they had no option whatever but to find absolutely against Mr. Quilliam on all points. They ordered the defend- ant to pay is. 6d. and costs (including an advocate's fee).

There were also summonses against Thomas Dale, Priestley-street; Georgeßroadhurst. Brick-street; and Jonathan Broadhur.st,_Bostock-street.

The magistrates deCided to adjourn them for 14 days in order thatlMr. Quilliam might have an opportunity of appealing against the 4ecision in the first case, if he desired. If he decided not to appeal, the tolls would be paid.

The Prevention and Treatment of Cancer.

In view of the increasing prevalence of cancer, the committee of the Liverpool Cancer Research-deem it their duty to call attention to what may be done to combat this increase.

No specific cure for cancer is yet known, but, as with consumption, the earlier the treatment is commenced the greater is the chance for its success. Unfortunately a large number of people put off seeking the advice of their doctor instead of going to him immediately they notice anything wrong. It may be pointed out that certain organs are more often affected than others. In men, cancer of the gullet, stomach, and intestines; in women, cancer of the breast and womb, account for about two-thirds of all cases of cancer. There are, of course, many other only slight ailments of these organs. The committee feel that they cannot too strongly state how important it is that the disease should be recognized at the earliest possible moment. This recognition can only be attained by the patient going to his or her doctor for examination at the very begin- ning of illness.

_ Cancer Research Laboratory, The University, Liverpool, July, 1904.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ALLEGED VIOLENT ASSAULT AT THE NEW WATERWORKS. At the Warrington. Occasional Court on Saturday, Erasmus Barnhum, engineman, Croft, was charged with having unlawfully wounded Patrick Doran,a blacksmith at Southworth-with-Croft. on June 27th.—Mr. T. S. Steel prosecuted, and said the parties were employed on the new Corporation waterworks at Croft. On the date in question a crane would not work properly and Doran made a suggestion. His remarks were taken excep- tion to by the prisoner, and words passed between them. Eventually, Barnhum seized a spade, deliberately raised it in the air, and struck Doran on the head with one corner of the implement. Doran had been in the hands of Dr. Sephton ever since, the wound being a very severe one.—ln his evidence the prosecutor said that after assaulting him with the spade, prisoner struck him in the face with his fist. —Prisoner was remanded until Monday next, bail being allowed.

A BAD CASE OF CRUELTY TO A HORSE.—Alfred Jeffs. farmer, Little Leigh, was charged with causing a horse to be worked whilst it was in an unfit condition. Thomas Curbishley, a carter, was charged with working it whilst in that condition.—Superintendent Ennion stated that he saw Curbishley in charge of a cart laden with macadem. Matter was oozing from under the sad- dle, and when he saw the horse he told the defendant to stop while be examined it. On the offside there was a long and deep wound in which one could put a hen egg. It was the worst case of cruelty he had ever witnessed. There was also another wound about the size of the palm of witness's hand, but it was not dircharging.— Corroborative evidence was given by a witness named Holland. Jeffs said he had been working at Cuddington and not noticed the condition of the animal.—Defend- ants were lined 10s. and costs each.

Expenditure Increasing.

A. meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. Brooks pre- sided, and there were also present: .Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. INCREASED. EXPEND IT URE FOR M A INTENANCE.

Mr. S. DAVIES called attention to the charges for expensive medicines and extra medical fees in the Newton and Haydock districts. He considered them exorbitant: He noticed in the Earlestown doctor's bill several items for difficult cases, and he would like to know whether in cases which were not difficult, the ordinary fees were always charged. Perhaps the mem- bers of the Newton Relief Committee would be more alive to their duty. _

Mr. THOMPSON called attvtion to the laundry. He noticed that a certain amount of washing was being sent out and that on June 25th, washing was done outside the house at a cost of £3 6s. He understood that all the washing was now done inside the House...

The CHAIRMAN explained that the washing was sent out owing to a delay in fixing one of the machines in the lAundry. ME BILLINGE asked whether Bradford's hadnow com- pleted their contract, and the Chairman replied in the affirmative.

Mr. BILLEiGE said the Clerk had called attention to the fact that the cost of maintenance was £7OO in excess of the corresponding half year. He would like to know whether the cost of the laundry equipment was not included in the half year's expenditure. A return ought to be presented showing the average expenditure per head in the schools and in the house.

The CLERK said the Master and the Superintendent would supply the information. The extra expenditure in connection with the laundry had been paid out of current revenue.

Mr. BILLINGE said in that case the cost of mainten- ance had been practically the same.

The CHAIRM-AN said the number in the Workhouse had been much greater than in the previous half-year. Mr. A. J. JOLLY observed that any discussion on the figures would be quite futile until they were carefully analysed.

The CLERK suggested that it would be well to have an account from each district, showing the increase made in out-relief. The officers would,no doubt, comply with the request and it would be desirable information before estimating the expenditure for the ensuing half-year.

HOUSE COMMITTEE. THE ACCOMMODATION QUESTION,

At a meeting of this committee, held on July Bth, in connection with the congested state of the house and the offer of the Tarvin Union, it wasi resolved to specially sit on Friday to again consider the resolution submitted by the sub-committee appointed to deal with the matter. The nursing deputation submitted their report and the subject was deferred pending some de- velopments that were in hand. The master having directed attention to the high prices paid for store pigs which tended to considerably diminish the profits it was resolved to empower Mr. Mather to have a free hand in the future in the purchasing of the pigs all exr penses in connection with such purchases to be met out o the master's petty cash.

AN INVENTORY OF THE HOUSE AND ITS COST The CHAIRMAN formally gave notice that the sub- committee appointed to inquire into the inventory recently made, with the view of ascertaining what re- muneration should be paid for the work done, would recommend at the next meeting that £25 be given to Mr. Awbery for his services in connection with the inventory. Mr. THOMPSON asked for more information.

_ . The CHAIRMAN said that some 18 months ago it was decided to have an inventory taken of the whole house and infirmary, and that extra remuneration be given for the work. The Clerk's department repudiated any liability to make the inventory, as did also the house department, and the Board decided that the inventory should be taken by the Master and the remunerativ assessed by a committee consisting of Mrs. Clay, Mr. J. .Tolley and himself. The inventory was never made by the latc, Master and the duty devolved upon Mr. Awbery. The work had been completed, and the sub- committee had gone carefully into the matter. The CLERK said the matter arose in this way. The I auditor complained that a satisfactory inventory was I not put before him. The inventory which was made many years ago was not only imperfect. but had become entirely wrong. Furniture had been taken from one part of the house to put in another, whilst some furni- ture had been worn out and replaced. The Clerk could not provide w inventory of the furniture in the house, I and the que.Aion arose whether some person used to., valuing furniture should be employed for the purpose. He thought Mr. Hickman's firm was spoken of. After consideration it was found that the inventory could be properly made by the Master and Matron. Before the work could be undertaken, the late Master left' and the duty had fallen upon Mr. Awbery. He had had the assistance of three or four persons. The Board would have an opportunity of seeing the inventory at the next meeting. It was a work that involved a large amount of time. Mr. THOMPSON said the resolution had better state that the extra remuneration was to cover all the ex- penses or those who had assisted would be asking for something extra. Mr. TIICKMAN thought that as it was only a notice of motion the discussion was out of place. There was no doubt that the resolution authorising the inventory was passed but he thought the Clerk was wrong in saying that he (Mr. Hickman) was spoken to about it. The CLERK: It was spoken of you, not to you. The Board then rose.

INTERESTING MEDICAL CASE.

SURREY COUNTY FURNISHES MATTER FOR THOSE INTERESTED IN KIDNEY.DISEASES.

-- - Surrey County furnishes a story that is of unusual interest to the general public, and more particularly to the medical profession. The story is the experience of Mr. L. D. Barrow, of Compton, Nr. Guildford, and as told by that gentlemanhimself, is as follows "Some time ago I caught a severe cold from leaning against hot steam pipes. I was attended by a doctor and tried various remedies, but without effect. I grew worse and suffered from Palpitation of the Heart, and my appetite left me. With the least exertion I grew tired. Dark circles came under my eyes and they were puffed and swollen. "I used 60 perspire frequently, sometimes I would turn dizzy, occasionally I was low spirited and de- pressed, and at times I was troubled with Neuralgia. My sight was affected and specks would float before my eyes • but the worst symptom in my case was a dark gravelly sediment in the kidney secretions. "I heard of Dodd's Kidney Pills and decided to give them a trial. lam glad I did so, for I derived great benefit from them."

TH GOVERNMENT AND THE LIQUOR TRAFFIC. A National Crisis —Call to Prayer.

The following has been issued by the President of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches to the ministers and members of the Free Churches of Eng- land and Wales : Dear Brethren and Friends.—At this hour of awful crisis, when the Government, deaf to every voice of remonstrance and appeal, is hastening to give to the Liquor Traffic a security of tenure which it has never before possessed, and to rivet on the neck of our cities, towns and villages a well- nigh intolerable yoke, as we have no resource left but to appeal with one voice to God. Surely our union in the most sacred bonds, as Free Church people, furnishes an opportunity for such united intercession as our forefathers never enjoyed. The Free Church Council movement bands us together not only for high spiritual work, but to defend our country from the infliction of great moral wrongs; amongst these we cannot but count the endowment of the Liquor Trade as one of the most flagrant. I beg to suggest therefore (and in doing this I have the concurrence of my beloved friend Dr. Horton, the President-Elect of the National Coun- cil) that next Sunday evening, the 17th inst, after the service in all our churches, chapels and mission halls, we should call our people together for united prayer, asking that even now the Government's Licensing proposals may be brought to nought. In some places the following day might likewise be consecrated to the purpose of intercessory prayer on behalf of our nation; for this purpose the fellowship of the clergy and people of the Church of England, who sympathise with us in this matter—and there are maq—might be sought and obtained. Forgive the directness and urgency of my appeal but the crisis is momentous and the need im- perative.—Yours sincerely, F. B. MEYER. (President of the National Free Church Council), Memorial Hall, E.C.

Child Drowned at Dutton. lAt 5.30 on Monday afternoon Elizabeth Millington, . aged five years, of Dutton Lodge Cottages, Dutton,was walking along the Canal Bank, at Dutton, with another girl named Clara Holms, also aged five years. Millington was trying to reach some rushes, when she fell into the water. Clara Holme tried to get her out, but as she did not succeed she gave the alarm. The body was dragged for and has since been recovered.

WARRRINGTON.

Sunday School Teachers' Association.

The -me.nbers of this Association held their quarterly meeting on Saturday at the Rectory. The grounds were open to visitors at three o'clock, and various games were provided. Tea was served in Fairfield Schools at 5.30, and in the-evening a short service was held in the Parish Church, when an interesting address was given to a good congregation by the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross, vicar of Stretton. Fire in Longford-street. The explosion of a paraffin lamp caused a fire at the house of Robert Ward, Earl-street, off Longford-street, shortly after ten o'clock on Monday night.. The Fire Brigade turned out, but on arrival found that the flames had been extinguished by neighbours. Ward was slightly injured, and considerable damage was done to furniture and pictures. Fortunately the flames were confined to the front room in which they broke out.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Death of Mr. Arthur Morris.

We regret to announce the death of Mr. Arthur Morris, who was well known in Warrington although for two or three years he had lived at Norton, near Worcester. The deceased was in the employ of the Warrington Slate Company Limited for a number of years, and on severing his connection with the firm carried on business in this town as a coal and coke merchant. He was prominently identified with St. Alban's Roman Catholic Church. Mr. Morris had suffered a long and painful illness, the cause of death being cancer. The interment took place at Norton, the last rites being performed by one of the priests from Worcester who had been in constant attendance on the deceased during his illness.

GARLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. St. John Ambulance Review. The North Eastern County Corps review, held last year in Warrington, took place on Saturday at Todmorden under delightful circumstances. The town nestling at the foot of some very finely wooded hills, which rise to a great height, presented a pretty picture. The streets had a very old world look about them, neither 'busses nor electric cars yet having made their appearance. A capital display of flags and mottoes was made all along the route of the pro- cession. Some 200 nursing sisters were entertained during the morning by the Mayoress, and the district staff were hospitably entertained by the Mayor. At 3 p.m. headed by two bands, the procession pro- ceeded through the town, everybody turning out to see such an unusual sight, while the local firemen, assisted by various ladies and gentlemen, were busy with collection boxes in aid of the Corps funds. After the march past, which was done in grand style, the nursing sisters leading, the Deputy Commissioner Colonel Trimble, C.M.G. made an inspection of the parade. Following this, came tests in bandaging and a supposed railway accident. Marks were given for the smartest appearance on parade, in conjunction with their individual work, at the supposed railway accident. In this Warrington tied with another Corps, and after a further test, Warrington received 3rd prize, consisting of an illuminated certificate, and ambulance material. The team which went earlier in the day to defend the Warrington Cup, got 3rd prize, the cup going to Briarfield. The party from War- rington numbered two officers, three sergeants and 16 men. The return journey was commenced at 8.11 and home was reached at 10.15 p.m. after a most enjoyable afternoon.

"Some Dangerous , Tendencies of Modern Life."

An address on this subject was delivered by the Rev. H. Dunnico at the open meeting of the Warring- ton P.S.A. Society on Sunday. He said that amongst many things he had noticed a tendency to abuse liberty. One of the most conspicuous features of modern life was the growth of the democratic spirit and the advance of liberty. This growth had also its dangerous as well as its promising aspects. Liberty was a glorious thing, but it was so frequently misunderstood and misinterpreted. Liberty was understood by some people to be merely the oppor- tunity of doing what one pleased, but such a con- ception was a fatally false one and indicated absolute bondage. Churches and ministers did not possess the authority they once did, and perhaps they were none the worse on that account. Still all this had its .dangers, because emancipation from outward restraints, no matter how imperfect those restraints were, led to a Lew and worse bondage unless there was a voluntary allegiance to the highest and noblest laws of life. Another tendency was to make more and more of society and less and less of the individual. This was, of course, a healthy, and a hopeful sign so long as it stood to condemn sel- fish isolation and suggested co-operation,sympathy and fellowship. If there was one lesson this century had taught us more than another it was the solidarity of the human race and of all with God. But this tendency also had its dangers in so far as there seemed to be a general weakening of the sense of individunl respon- sibility. Social and philosophic kindnesses which were being taught in certain quarters obscured,even if they did not deny, the individual freedom and personal res- ponsibility, and he warned his hearers to beware of such doctrines, because they were pernicious and harmful. The speaker also referred to the increasing desire for excitement and self-indulgence and social dissipations. He held no Puritanic view of amuse- ments, but when the pursuit of amusement became the chief passion. of a life, then they had turned into a curse what God intended to be a blessing. In con- clusion Mr. Dunnico referred to the existing noisy, rampant, but shallow atheism. They might rest assured, however, that the so-called new Atheism was as old as Lucretius and it was crude as it was old. The Christian religion was as well attested to-day as ever, if not more so. Mrs. Everness, of the Salvation Army, contributed two solos.

AWHEEL TO BEESTON, Mr. John Naylor Entertains the Warrington Cycling Club.

It is the good fortune of the Warrington Cycling Club to receive a cordial invitation each year to visit the charming Cheshire retreat of their President, Mr. John Naylor, at Beeston. Himself an ardent cyclist,. Mr. Naylor esteems it a pleasure to entertain the wheelers, and President's Day, as the occasion is popularly known, is looked forward to with perhaps greater delight than any of the season's outings.. Favoured with glorious weather, Saturday—the President's Day of 1901—will be remembered as a grand success. True the broiling sun shining from a clottdless sky made cycling warm work indeed, but it was sufficient compensation that the picturesque country side was seen at its 'best. Nearly a hundred members and friends—including a good sprinkling of the fair sex—journeyed to Beeston, and most of them covered the 221 miles by road. As usual, a small contingent travelled by rail via Chester, but the wheelers congratulated themselves upon having made the best choice. They had a delightful spin through the richly wooded area whieq embraces part of the far-famed Delatnere Forest. The hedgerows were bright %with the delicate tints of the wild rose and honeysuckle, and here and there clusters of lovely foxgloves added more striking dashes of colour. Stiff hills had to be negotiated, but there were exhilarating spells of free-wheeling. Pleasingly situated on a hill side, Beeston Towers command a beautiful view, whilst hard by the olden/tie stand, out majestically. Most of the cyclists rode to the gates in a body, and Mr. and Mrs. Naylor were waiting to receive them. The travellers were rather dust-stained, but a cooling wash speedily refreshed them. Then they were called to the pretty tennis court, where an excellent tea was served. It was a merry party and all were full of appreciation of Mr. Naylor's hospitality. The meal over, the visitors were free to ramble about the house and grounds. The exquisite carving with which the rooms abound came in for much admiration. Several cueists indulged in their favourite pastime in the well- appointed hill iard room, and trundlers betook themselves to the bowling green. The treat of the evening was an at fresco concert which gave the utmost pleasure to all. The Vkfarrington Cycling Club has some capital musical talent at its command, and the programme did the artistes great credit. There were songs by Mrs. Arthur Booth, Miss Pemberton, Mr. A. Fieldhouse,Mr. W. H. Treland, and Mr. Peter Bate. The voices of Mrs. Booth and Miss Pemberton blended beautifully in "The Venetian boat song," and another capital duet was " The moon hath raised" by Messrs. Ireland and Fieldbouse. The duties of accompanists were discharged by Mrs. Booth and Mr. Bate. A dance on the lawn was an enjoyable wind-up to the gathering, Mr. Finch supplying the music. Before the visitors left, ..Mr. D. Hodge, the captain of the club, voiced the general sentiment in proposing a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Naylor for their hospitality. It was, he said, one of the features of the club to be enter- tained by Mr. Naylor, who was an ardent cyolist. He was sure Mr. Naylor was very pleased to see so many cyclists at his home, but the vote of thanks was quite inadequate to such generosity. The vote was accorded with enthusiastic cheers and musical honours. mr. pa Davies also thanked the host and hostess and said that day was regarded as a red-letter day in the annual history of the club. They had been fortun- ate in once more having what they called Naylor's weather. He trusted the conditions would be equally favourable next year. In returning thanks, Mr. Naylor said that Mrs. Naylor and himself were very pleased to receive a visit from the club, and, as Mr. Davies had said, this was regarded as a red-letter day. No visitors to Beeston Towers were more welcome nwsn hads m e nbaenedn thagnladhihsisfienlslterwnettieo ctharriesdwooumtewnitahndrehfee wasrece to the presence of the ladies. He looked upon cycling as the finest form of exercise, and not only so, but it enabled one to see the country. It was certainly a good exercise for the ladies. He need hardly assure them that he was looking forward with much pleasure to their next meeting. (Applause). Having taken leave of the host and hostess the cyclists turned their faces homewards. The evening was refreshingly cool and a splendid ride to town concluded an outing which will long be remembered with pleasure.

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

Monthly Meeting. The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held in Bold-street Wesleyan schools on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Mr. J. Podmore, jun. There was a good attendance of delegates.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR REPRESENTATION COMM/TTEE:

The SECRETARY (Councillor Stott) reported that he had received a copy of the quarterly report of the National Labour Representation Committee, showing the new affiliations during the quarter to number 14,470.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE COMPANIES V. MUNICIPAL ENTERPRISE.

The SECRETARY stated that he received a circular from a committee representing the Co-operative Society and the trade union branches in the Newcastle district with respect to the efforts of a small private company owning eight miles of tramways to obtain compulsory running powers over the Newcastle Corporation system which was twenty three miles in length.ln consequence he wrote to Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P. asking him to use his influence in Parliament to prevent the powers being granted, and to support a motion to delete the compulsory clauses in the Company's Bill. He (Councillor Stott) stated that he looked upon the matter as one affecting workers and ratepayers all over thecountry, and he felt that if the request were allowed it would vitally injure or place in jeopardy all municipal tramway services. Mr. Pierpoint did not acknowledge the letter. When the matter came before the House, the Bill was sent back to the committee for reconsideratinn. Mr. Pier- point was asked by the Electricity and Tramways Cora- imitteeof the Corporation to oppose the Bill. In spite of the requests of the Trades Council and Town Council be voted in favour of permission being given to the company, whereas Colonel Pilkington and Sir H. Seton Karr opposed the recommendation. That was, he thought, the first occasion within his recollection where they found Mr. Pierpoint voting in opposition to the two Tory members from the neighbouring 3onstit- uencies.

- The CHAIRMA.N said there was no doubt that municipal enterprise would be met with these kind of proposals as, years went on. The exponents of private enterprise for individual profit would always tryto fight municipalization. As working people they had a great deal to hope, for from municipal effort, and it should be their duty to, thwart the efforts of those who put private enterprise before public welfare. The Secretary's action was endorsed. CORT'OR!kTION_ CONTRACTS. •

Notice was received of the annual meeting of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of Trades and Labour Councils. to be held on July 30th. A resolution will be submitted calling attention to the status of a firm holding contracts for police clothing for many Lancashire and Cheshire authorities, and proposing that the Federation Executive be instructed to make such inyestigations and to take such steps as would ensure proper recognition of the fair contracts clause supposed to apply to the contracts in questions. Another resolu- tion urges the, necessity of meetings of public bodies being held in.the evening insteat. of during the day as as present. The CHAIRMAN instanced how Councillor Stott would have to lose work if he attended the meetings of Cor- poration committees of which he was a member, and he contended that this was unfair.

Councillor STOTT said he had been making inquiries respecting a firm which received Corporation clothing contracts. As far as he could gather, this was the only ,firm of the kind in that particular town, and the condi* tions of labour were very bad. The standing order of the Town Council said the recognized rate for the district "should be paid, but if there was only one firm in the district the Council could not interfere, although the conditions might be bad. The CHAIRMAN complained of the bad fit of clothing supplied to a tramway man, and Other delegates considered the hats were much too heavy to be worn in summer. _ _ _ _

It Was decided that the Executive Committee should consider the fair wage question, with a view to drawing up a new clause to be submitted to the Council.

FLINT GLASS MAKERS' DISPUTE.

Mr. E. W. LEICESTER, a representative of the local branch of the National Flint Glass Makers' Society, attended and explained at length the circumstanecs leading to the recent lock-out. The Council endorsed the Society's appeal, and the secretary was instructed to forward copies to each branch.

In the appeal' it is stated that"the employers have de- manded and locked us out for a deduction in our wages of 20 per cent. We have met them twice in committee and we offered to concede a sum equal to an advance in wages we received some years ago, for beyond this we cannot go without reducing ourselves to a slavish posi- tion,and knowing full well that reducing wages will not in the slightest degree improve the trade or take. one unemployed member off the roll. We hold it is our right to fight and struggle against the outrageous and unwarrantable, demand of the employers." The lock out in Manchester and Warrington,it is stated, will put out of employment 150 men, who are members of the trade federation but not entitled to benefit at present on ac- count of only being members six months.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
Vik-COGOA MAKES MEN CHEERFUL.

The work of Mr. H. Hockley, of 5, Lorrimore-ros,d, Walworth, London, compelled him to be out in all winds and weathers, and until six weeks ago he used to get very depressed and always seemed tired and weary. ===== 0002601_19040716_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Councillor Stott and the Rev. H. Dunnico.

On Sunday evening at the close of a lecture by Miss Mabel Parker Grant at Bridge Foot, Councillor Stott made a lengthy reference to certain statements of the Rev. H. Dunnico's. The first statement he objected to was one to the effect that "drink " was the cause of more unnecessary evil, vice, bigamy, and degradation than all other causes put together including our present competitive system. The second statement was to the effect that the teachings of the Socialistic party w•re not based upon Christianity." The first statement was untrue, said Councillor Stott, and the second was a misrepre- sentation, and they challenged Mr. Dunnico to a public discussion. Mr. Dunvico, who happened to be in the crowd, asked permission to speak, but Councillor Stott refused. The crowd however took the matter,up and eventually Mr. Dunnico mounted the box. He stated very briefly that he did not remember exactly what the papers had reported him as saying, but to all intents and purposes they had reported him correctly, and he accepted the challenge on one condi- tion—that his opponent must be a: resident in the town. He objected on principle to meeting outside agitators. This condition Councillor Stott and one or two demurred at, whereon Mr. Dunnico said if they could not find one to meet him he would meet two or three as the case might be. Mr. Dunnices acceptance was loudly applauded by the crowd.

" Buy and Edith are sisters, four and five years old respectively.
