"Two little fellows like me and Tommy break it down !" ex- claimed the boy. "Yes, you will." "Au' last night when that Mr. Smithkins was calling on you, an you got tired sittin' on the porch, and " " Never mind, Willie," she interrupted, hastily. "Maybe it's all right, after all." Softly shone the subdued light of the solitary gas-jet in the drawing-room of the Bilderback mansion and soft was the voice of the abashed young Erasmus Shackelford, who sat on the edge of his chair, mopped his heated face, and smiled with a kind of paper-mache smile at the entranc- ing young woman, in the dazzling aureola of whose auburn hair he had fluttered in agoniaing captivity for months and months. "Miss Viola," he said, clearing his throat, and speaking with Avery in- fliction of a man about to say something, " you will not be surprised, I presume, if I—if I express the—the feeling, the—the opinion, as it were, that —that it's pretty hot this evening?" Erasmus gave his face another frenzied wipe with his hand- kerchief, and subsided into palpitating silence. "No," replied Miss Viola, with a smile that brought a large and ecstatic lump of something or other up into his throat, "I am not surprised, Mr. Shackelford. You made the same observation earlier in the evening." "Y-yes," he gasped. "I believe I did. It—it was not an entirely new re- mark. In fact, it was a kind of a—of a chestnut, I suppose." The agitated youth made another effort to crush down the lump in his throat. " And I—l feel, Miss Bil—Viola—as if I were a —4 kind of—of observation myself that was get- ting tiresome. Haven't I been—been observed here a little too often? Da I seem to be a—a chestnut"—and as he moved his handkerchief over his glowing face in tremulous jabs, his voice took on a despairing sound—" a sort of—of roasted chestnut ? " "No Erasmus," slowly answered the maiden. " When a chwitnut is roasted it pope." The conscientious historian i 4 bound to record the fact that at this point Erasmus immediately DOPPed.

A pipe line 280 miles long, built for the purpose of conveying oil from the Kern River district to a ship- ping point on San Francisco Bay,was recently completed and opened for service' when a very unexpected diffi- culty was encountered. The oil is so heavy that it was five days travelling the first thirty-seven miles. It has been decided to make the experiment of heating the oil to about 120 degrees, and at the same time the number of pumping stations will be greatly increased.

The Social Problem.

Si r,—Your'correspoedent resents'my hard and straight hitting but when a person enters the arena in such an arrogant and flippant manner as "P. 8. he must be pre pared to accept the inevitable consequences. I have already been compelled to accuse "P. 8. of being illogical and narrow-minded and I now add an additional 'accusatory viz., unconsistency. Moreover your correspondent's latest letter is full of assertions altogether opposed to that which is tame and which reveal a lamentable ignorance of the principles of the party of which he professes to be a member. However no good purpose can be served by continuing this dis- cussion and I think it'might come to an end. Of course if "P. 8. wishes me to point out the bad logic and the narrow-minded inconsistent and incorrect statements in his letter I will do so, but not otherwise. "P. 8. I think will on consideration recognise the wisdom in my suggestion; at this critical juncture in our nation's history the progressive forces cannot afford to be divided.—Yours sincerely, HERBERT DUNNICO.. July 15th.

Sir,—May I be permitted a few lines in answer to ths ev. H. Dunnico's two last replies to my previoue letters. Mr. Dunnico says that I asked why he publicly identified himself with the Liberal party. What Ido ask is this. Why does Mr. Dunnico remain officially a member of the Liberal party while at the same time being the head of the Social Service League? Is it in the best interests of the league that be does so? Before the Social Brotherhood was formed his position in the Liberal party was all right, in fact, for that matter it may be so now, only as I say the question is,— is it best? And with regard to the use of the word " God " I am not prepared to modify what I have said to any great extent. The Non-Christian section of the league are entitled to consideration and if we get too much religion and theology—well there might be trouble. We shall see appearing in our local papsrs the following startling headings—" Social Ser- vice League Meeting" "Rowdy Theological Dis- cussion" "Exciting Scenes." Do you know, sir I do not profess to be intolerant or narrow-minded and indeed if history be true I do believe that my Christian friends (not the "very advanced Christians' of course) have always been noted for their "tolerance and broadmindedness." "The gospel of Jesus Christ" says the reverend gentleman "is beyond doubt the finest ethic 1h the entire sphere of moral science and is therefore the best political economy." According to our preachers we are to believe that Jesus knew everything that would happen under the sun and that this teaching would apply to all times and all countries. And Ido not know very much about it being "beyond doubt" the finest ethic As for the "best political economy" this is all—shall I say—rot. I don't suppose Jesus bothered much with political economy and if He were here now he might probably consider political economy too big a subject. Apply Jesus' teaching about taking no thought for the morrow to modern 'life—and then—think. We want no religious movements, we have had enough. However I do not wish to quarrel on religious grounds. Perhaps as H. G. Wells would say, I am "anticipating" trouble. To the Rev. H. Dunnico I say in conclusion, let us be reasonable and tolerant.—Yours &c.,______ _

ONE INTERESTED.

AN "INTOLERABLE NUISANCE" AT ARPLEY.

Health Committee Prosecute.

Albert Mynekyme, butcher, Market-street, was sum- moned at the Borough Police Court yesterday, for having by carrying on the business of glue making at Arpley caused an effluvium which was a nuisance and injurious to the health of the inhabitants of the district. —Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecuted for the Health Committee, and Mr. Steel defended. Mr. Wilson said the magistrates had the power to in- flict a penalty not exceeding £5 and not less than £2. In the early part of this year the defendant applied to the Health Committee for permission to establish an offensive trade, and in March the committee granted permission on defendant promising to carry out the requirements of the Medical Officer of Health. Dr. Gornall visited the works and submitted his require- ments in writing to the defendant,who promised to carry them out. From time to time Dr. Gornall had inspected the premises and had found that without exception de- fendant had failed to fulfil the requirements. In conse- quence of so many complaints having been received by the Health Committee, some from persons residing half a mile from the works, the Medical Officer was instructed to report specially upon the works. As the result of the report and the certificate which he gave proceedings were instituted. There could be no doubt that the nuisance arising front the works was simply intolerable. People living in Wilson-Patten-street, Wilderspool-road and neighbourhood, complained bitterly, and he asked the Bench to inflict a substantial penalty. Dr. Gornall stated that on June 17th he was passing along New-road when he smelled a very strong odour which was undoubtedlya nuisance to the neighbourhood and injurious to health. There was no doubt whatever that the smell arose from the glue works. In answer to Mr. Steel, witness said he had heard the works were ownod by a company. They were the only works of the kind in Warrington. Mr. Steel said the works had only been open for three months, and the company had hardly had time to get them into working order. He suggested that the case should be adjourned for a month. During the hot weather an offensive smell had arisen from an accumula- tion of bones, but these were almost used up, and de- fendant was not getting another supply. The Mayor : We are not inclined to let you poison the neighbourhood for another month. Mr. Mynekyme gave an undertaking not to. carry on the trade during the month, and on that understanding the case was adjourned.

Stockton Heath and the Warrington Light Railway. The Widening of Old London Road. Sir,--My statement that it was expressly ruled by the Light Railway Commissioners that the Stockton Heath Parish Council had a right to appear, may be verified by a reference to the report of the proceedings at the Inquiry in the "Warrington Guardian" of March sth, 1902. So far from the appearance of the Parish Council being a- privilege allowed through the courtesy of the Town Clerk, it may be stated that although the Parish Council appeared on three several occasions, it was reserved for the Counsel of the Warrington Corporation to raise the objection that the Parish Council could not be recognized, and it was in answer to -this objection that Colonel Boughey, the chairman ruled that, " the Parish Council had a right to be heard." Nobody has ever said (because it has nothing to do with the point under discussion) that the Light Railways Act recognised a Parish Council, though as a matter of fact that Act does recognise a Parish Council—for ex- ample section 24 of the Act which provides for an amending order being obtained on the application of "any authority "—words which in the opinion of Mr. Steward (a barrister, late the secretary and now one of the Late Railway Commissioners) include a Parish Council. But it matters not for the purpose of my argument whether the Council appeared by right or by privilege so long as the fact that the Council did appear is admitted, as it now is, for this disposes of the implication intended to be conveyed by the Town Clerk's letter to Mr. England that because, (as he states) the Light Railways Act does not recog- nise a Parish Council therefore that Council was not in a position to obtain terms as to the road widening. As to the width of the road, it is sought to prove that my statement is wrong because the Parish Council is not named in the Order neither is the width of 50 feet named therein. Nobody ever said they were so named. But the name of the Parish Council Clerk is mentioned in the Order and the width of the Toad is defined by reference to "a plan signed by Edwin Bradshaw" who, as everybody here ought to know is the Clerk to the Stock- ton Heath Parish Council. Section 5 clause 1 of the Warrington Corporation Light Railways Order, 1903, is as follows :--- Before or concurrently with the construction of Railway (No. 1) the Corporation "shall widen the road upon which the same is to be laid "wherever it is shown on the plans that land is to be "acquired for that purpose to such extent that the "metalled portion of the road shall not be less than "thirty-five feet wide with a footpath not less than "five feet wide on each side thereof or such greater " width as is shown upon a plan signed by Edwin Brad- " shaw and deposited with the Board of Trade: Pro-i Two nomination papers have been handed in on behalf " vidsd that in places where the width available apt° the of Jacob Sankey, dairyman, Church-street. The Education t ontroversy. Registration—The First Duty of Free Churchman.

Sir,—E.rer since the Education Acts have been passed Free Churchmen have been told that they can find their remedy at the polling booth. The first step which Nonconformists can take in this matter is to see that their names appear on the register. It is often said that elections are won in the Registration Courts so that this is the moment when action should be taken. Claims should not be left to the last day, as there is always a possibility of a claim being mislaid or overlooked. The Free Church Council which last year unanimously determined to make education a test question at the -next general election, is now urging Free Churchmen to see that the names of those entitled to vote appear on the register now being prepared. Many a man loses the franchise through sheer lack of knowleege. Claim forms can always be obtained from the overseers or from the ordinary political organizations. There are many young men living at home who are entitled to claim a lodger vote, who might be put on the register if one enthusiastic man in each district were to make this his special business during the next few days. Amid the complications of the registration question, one or two simple facts may be remembered. Owner- ship claims must be sent in to the overseers by 20th July. Occupation and new lodger claims must be sent in by 20th August. Lodgers already on the register must put in afresh claim each year between 15th and 25th July. It is earnestly hoped that all Free Churchmen will give immediate attention to this matter.—Yours faith- fully,

THOMAS LAW. Organising Secretary National Council of Free Churches.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Representation of Warrington.

Sir,—Two correspondents, "W. Furness " and "Thomas Knowles," were good enough to take notice of me in last Saturday's paper. As the communications are very much alike, and mainly devoted to laudating Messrs; Crosfield and their works, I do not feel called upon to follow them. One or two points, however, may b noticed. On the assumption of his own apparently fertile imagination, "\V. Furness "insinuates that lam a setter up of strife among people. I should be •extremely sorry to deserve such a reputation, as I am, perhaps, as anxious as my censor himself to cherish peace and goodwill among my fellow men. But when great national wrongs are oppressing and grinding out the life of a large proportion of our working classes it is a mean and cowardly policy which looks on with half-averted eyes, and whines out peace, peace, where there is no peace. Had this been the craven attitude `of the noble Wilberforce, and his friends, in all probability the British nation would never have been called upon to pay twenty millions of money for the redemption of West Indian slaves. Certain it is that such an expen Mare would find small grace in the eyes of our present rulers who seem more disposed to promote than to suppress com- mercial dealing in human beings made after the image of God. Nothing but honest, earnest, and determined agitation will ever avail •to destroy and root out our infamous land and liquor laws, and reduce what is called capital to its proper place in the industrial world. The sneers of "Thomas Knowles" as to the impotence of the few labour members in Parliament are both ungenerous and premature, and can only be understood ,on the unsatisfactory supposition that he is a man prone to reason from insufficient data. What can a mere handful of humble working men do to oppose the will or thwart the purpose of six hundred polished members of the dominant aris- tocracy of England But the tables will be turned before the world is much older. The workers are be- ginning to realise that, as God's creatures, they have rights and privileges which have hitherto been denied to them through unjust laws and usages, and their faces are already set in the direction of better things. And when they shall have obtained the majority in Parliament to which they are justly entitled some of their traducers will have reason.to change their opinion. For my own part, lam sanguine enough to 'elieve that the natural love of fair play which distinguishes British -workmen would soon make its mark in a Parliament led and controlled by labour members. Further, from some of his remarks I infer, that "Thomas Knowles "looks favourably upon the Liberal candidate because he is a capitalist. I cannot do so for this reason. Divested of all sophistries, capital, whether derived ;from usury ,or from" Profit and Loss," is a premium levied by idle- ness upon industry, and often forms a disturbing ele- ment in the relations between employer and em- ployed.—Yours truly, July, 1901.

===== 0002601_19040716_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, July 16, 1904

In the Lil?eral programme, as Mr. Crosfteld had said, there were a great many burning and important ques- tions. In the Liberal arena there was an immense amount of work to be done, and two things were needed in Warrington,—unity and enthusiasm. If they could have the enthusiasm thrown into the political life of Warrington that they had thrown into that excursion victory was sure. (Applause.) They wanted to emancipate Warrington from Toryism. It had been under Tory rule long enough, and now they knew that every vote given to the Tories at the last election meant dear bread, tea, sugar, and meat. Hence politics had now become a home question. (Hear, hear.) The curious part of the present situation from the stand- point of their opponents was that consols had gone down and food had gone up. The expenses of the house- hold had increased and the income had diminished, and all who were in favour of low wages and dear food must vote for the Tories at the next election, but all who preferred higher wages and cheaper food must vote for the candidate who had addressed them that afternoon. (Cheers). They wanted from that moment to increase the solidarity of the ranks of Liberalism in the Borough from which they had come to multiply the number sf adherents and to increase the enthusiasm. (Hear, hear.) The speaker, in conclusion announced that next week Mr. Crosfleld, himself and one or two others, intended to address open-air meetings in Warrington at the street corners when they would open out some of these serious political question';. There never was a time when they had a Government that more thoroughly misrepresented the nation's views than the present one nor a member for Warrington who more thoroughly misrepresented the needs of Warrington than the present member who was supposed to represent the borough. The Government was disorganised and discredited and ought to be out of office. It was a question whether they were politically alive or dead. They were only united on one-question. It was the length of time they intended to continue to draw their salary. (Laughter). Address- ing the women Mr. Howes said if they had not votes they had the voters under their influence. It was said that those who rocked the cradle ruled the world, and those who were married had sufficient influence to make the voter do right on the polling day. (Cheers).

VOTES OF THANKS. ALDERMAN PIERPOINT AND WARRINGTON LIBERALISM.

Mrs. Jackson Holmes proposed and Mrs. (nay seconded, a vote of thanks to Mr. and Miss Crosfleld for their presence amongst them that day. (Applause.) Alderman Pierpoint said that Miss Crosfleld said to him a few minutes ago that he ought to remind them that very shortly there would be held in Warrington a gigantic Liberal bazaar and that she hoped they would endeavour to make it the magnificent success they made the last bazaar. Miss Crosfield suggested that all the ladies who were not attached to any of the working committees of the bazaar should make up their minds to work one article to be sold at the bazaar. That was a suggestion that should meet with their entire approval. (Hear, hear and applause.) He hoped the suggestion would bear good fruit. The motion was then put and carried and Mr. Crosfleld replied on behalf of his sister and him- self and congratulated the company on the beautiful weather which had been experienced. The most practi- cal and uaeful suggestion was mentioned that afternoon by Mr. Pierpoint on behalf of Miss Crosfleld. He hoped most earnestly that it would receive very favourable consideration. (Applause.) Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Howes said they had had the pleasure of listen- ing to Mr. Howes very frequently of late but not too often by any means. (Hear, bear.) The most interest- ing address he had given them that afternoon deserved their heartiest thanks. (Applause.) The motion was carried.

Mr. Howes replied and proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman (Alderman Pierpoint), who, he said, had been in the front of battle in the past and would be again. He was a man of a good political character, and he was sure they would all be able to do some valuable work between now and the next general election. (Applause). Miss Crosfield seconded the motion, and said she agreed very heartily with every word which Mr. Howes had said. It was a very great pleasure to her to second the vote of thanks. (Cheers.) The motion was carried._

The CHAIRMAN, in reply, said the present was a reminiscence of old times. He had grown grey in the service. He recollected working for Mr Peter Rylands and Mr. McMinnies. It was then all reioieing in War- rington and they looked back to th )se days with pride and pleasure. He had fought for Mr. William Crosfleld and Mr. John Crosfield—(cheers)—and he should have voted for any other Crostield if they had put him up as a Liberal in Warrington. (Cheers.) He had special plea- sure in saying he was quite ready to do what he was able on behalf of the latest of the Crosfleld's who had grown up amongst them. They knew nothing of Mr. Arthur Crosfield which was not good, and so long as he lived and they lived, he would always bear the same character. It was a standing disgrace to Warrington that the Tory party should have reigned so.long. but when they con- sidered the mighty forces at their disposal in the shape of the two great breweries and all the licensed houses of the town, they as Liberals mu3t own that they had a very hard, uphill fight to carry on. But surely Warring- ton had found its senses tst last, and if they did not win the next election they would all want shooting. (Laughter and cheers.) The gathering then dispersed.

LIBERAL WOMEN'S OUTING.'

A PARTY OF SEVEN HUNDRED AT EGGLESTON FERRY.

The Liberal ladies of Warrington are nothing if not energetic. They raise money when it is required for party purposes by means of bazaars, and they maintain a sturdy Liberal interest all the year round amongst themselves and those Of their friends whose convictions do not already lie with the other side, by means of lectures and meetings of various kinds. Once a twelvemonth they 'have an outing, at which a few representatives of that other species, known sometimes as malignant man, are permitted to act the humble part of visitors whose company is welcome but whose presence is by no means a necessity. The numbers attending this annual social function have gradually increased, but this year the outing assumed abnormal proportions, and no fewer than 700 ladies and a few gentlemen participated. The programme for the day was to start from Bank Quay Station about 11-30, spend a little time in Chester, then gl up the Dee to Eccleston Ferry and visit Eaton Hall gardens. Afterwards sit down to a substantia tea and listen to a little speechifying at the close. To carry out such a delightful programme on so extensive a scale required careful arrangement, and it was in this connection that the mere man played his part to perfection. In Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. J. Howes, junr., and Mr., F. Plumpton, a trio were found who devoted themselves whole-heartedly to the work, and they had the satisfaction of knowing at the close of the day that they had won the golden opinions of the fair sex for the coinpleteness with which they had arranged everything appertaining to the pic-nic. The scene at the railway station was an animated and unusual one. Never before has such a large com- pany of Warrington ladies entrained for a pic-nic of this kind. The limited accommodation of the railway platform was taxed to the utmost. One portion of the special train which had been engaged, lay in the bay and was afterwards hooked on to the other portion which was drawn along the main line. The ladies, as may readily be imagined, made a picturesque group, There were graceful young women, divinely tall and most divinely fair, clad in creations of the loom and the milliners art which defy adequate description, there were middle-aged women dressed inimore sombre hue, and matrons of still more advanced years, who were not able to enter into the day's merry-making with the zest of former years. Beautifully fine weather favoured the outing, the sun shining brightly from a blue and almost unclouded sky. As the train sped on its journey to ChAter the merry noise of the reap- ing machine was heard in the adjacent fields and the air was filled with the perfume of new- mown hay. Chester was reached about noon and Messrs. Crosfield's band heralded the arrival of this great party of Liberal ladies by striking up a lively tune, and leading the way through Grosvenor park to the boating stage on the river side. All Capner's steam and electric launches had been char- tered for the occasion but they were found insufficient to convey the whole of the passengers at one journey, and consequently some were delayed at the landing stage until the steamers returned. The sail along the winding reaches of the Dee was greatly appreciated.. There was always some charming scene for the pas- sengers to feast their eyes upon—palatial residences , with grounds sloping gently to the Dee side, verdant Ifields with richly wooded slopes,or the boating on the river itself.

Immediately on arrival at Eccleston Ferry, the party was greeted by Mr. Arthur Crosfield and Miss Crosfield. A pleasant stroll through Eaton Park was followed by a visit to the gardens where horticulture was seen to perfection. Tea was served in tents and tea houses on the opposite side of the river by Mr. Partington, and the men in charge of the ferry were kept busily employed in transferring the party from one side of the stream to the other. The whole party was accommodated at one sitting and an excellent knife and fork tea was served in a manner which gave unqualified satis- faction. A few speeches were afterwards delivered and the' homeward jovney was commenced in good time. Warrington vfas safely reached about ten o'clock and the ladies seemed pleasantly surprised to find a great many of the male sex waiting patiently in the Booking Hall and outside the station, ready to accompany them home.

THE SPEECHES.

The Chairman observed that it always had been a rule at these meetings of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association that a few remarks should be made with reference to the cause that had brought them together. (Hear, hear.) He was one of the oldest frequenters—he was almost ashamed to say it—to these very happy gatherings and he noticed that they kept growing year by year. (Hear, hear.) He was rather inclined to think that the growth in the numbers of their gathering was in some measure an,instance of the growth of Liberalism in Warrington. (Cheers,) They might be allowed to introduce politics slightly and on that occasion they had an opportunity, of asking some of their friends to say a few words. (Hear, hear.) MR. ===== 0002601_19040716_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POINT.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield, who had a hearty reception, said Mr. Pierpoint had alluded to the interference that politics might make with a pleasurable afternoon in the country and partly on account of that delicate hint and partly because they wanted to organise a pleasant trip down the river, he was not going to detain them for more than a moment or two. It was not because there was not plenty to be said, it was not because that the great questions that they as politicians had at heart,did not concern Nomen every bit as much as they concerned men. (Hear, hear.) Whether it was the education of our children or the advantages we had in this country which he had had the opportunity of comparing with the disadvantages in other countries—the advantages of cheap clothing, cheap boots and cheap food—whether it was that question or the question of fair treatment of workingmen and equality before the law as between them and capitalists, or any of the other important questions—there were about half-a-dozen of them before the country at the present Moment—he was sure if they considered them they would feel that they concerned the welfare of householders as much, and therefore affected the happiness and the well-being of the mothers of households and of women as much—or he would say more than—they did the interests of the workpeople and the men in that assembly. (Cheers.) Therefore it was not because the questions did not concern the ladies or because they did not earnestly ask them to give them (the Liberals) a very powerful backing in the considerable amount of work that lay before them, but because of time and because they were mainly assembled for other purposes that afternoon that he did not Venture to detain them longer. Although he had addressed a few meetings in his time, he did not think he had ever heard politics talked under such picturesque surroundings as they had at Eccleston that afternoon. (Hear, hear.) It was very tempting—it was almost enough to make one go on—especially when he had in his ear the murmurs of the Dee, to remind them that they had the flowing tide with them. (Laughter and cheers.) They had been beating up stream for a great many years in Warrington. It bad not always been very encouraging; the skies had been a little bit beclowied at times, but now he was sure Mr. Alderman Pierpoint was perfectiy right when he said that the great numbers in which they were gathered together that day were an indication that at last they were going to wake up, and that they would give a very good account indeed of themselves whenever they were called upon to do so. (Cheers.) A great deal—an incalcul- able amount—depended upon the kind assistance and support which be was sure all present would give them. (Cheers.) He was delighted that before they separated on their homeward journey, they were going to listen to a few words from one of the finest and bravest and most consistent Liberals in the whole of the kingdom—Mr. Howes. (Cheers.) MR. HOWES ANDI THE PRIMROSE LEAGUE. AMUSING REFERENCE.

Mr. Joseph Howes in the course of a brief address said that Liberal organizations all over the country were now doing an enormous amount of work and cer- tainly as reformers they would be neglecting their duty if they did not rally the female forces to the standard of progress and improvement. On the other side they had a magnificently picturesque institution, the Primrose League made up of ladies, aristocratic and otherwise, whose duty at election times was to kiss the babies and inquire after the good men. (Laughter.) The differ- ence between a Women'S Liberal Association and the Primrose League was simply this—that one was political Ind the other was not. It was a pity that that beautiful flower the primrose had been taken as the badge of that institution. A friend of his wrote a few lines which he entitled "The degradation of the Primrose," and they read thus :

Poor little primrose, humble flower, Thine is a mournful story; What evil deed pray bast thou done Thus to be dubbed a Tory? Once thou was't loved for thine own sake By all who might come near thee; But since a noble lord pegged "out, A Liberal dare not wear thee. What is it that I hear thee say? With shyness in each dimple " They nlaim me for a Tory, sir Because lam so simple.'; (Loud laughter.)

THE PUPIL TEACHERS' CENTRE.

Report of the Education Sub-Committee.

The following report of the Sub-Committee appointed to consider the communication received from the Board of Education with reference to the pupil teachers' centre will be submitted at the Education Committee meeting on Monday next : "As a whole, the letter of the Board may be regarded as favourable, the Board being prepared to recognise the centre under Art. 11. of the pupil teachers' regulations. With respect to the Board's suggestions that the boys should be offered scholarships for admission to the Secondary school, this has already been done by the committee, though only at present as a temporary means, but it is hoped by next year to organise a more thorough system of scholarships. Your committee have already, in their advertisements for pupil teachers, intimated that the pupil teacherships are open to candidates from the Grammar, or any public Secondary or private school. _ .

With reference Co the curriculem, the recommenda- tion of the Inspectors that French and English sub- jects should hold a more prominent place than they do at present is to be carried out. It has been felt that the pupil teachers have been so poorly prepared when commencing their apprenticeships, that the attention of the teachers should be devoted to making them success- ful in the scholarship examination, and in view of the fact that few, if any, had done any French previous to commencing their apprenticeships, it was not thought advisable that they should do so at so late a period. This recommendatiion will be carried out when the pupil teachers have had a course of training in the Secondary school and, in order that this may be done, it is proposed to modify the curriculum of the preparatory pupil teachers with a view to devoting more time to the English and French subjects. With respect to the suggestion of the Inspectors as to the instruction in the art of teaching, the committee have already anticipated this suggestion, and the Inspectors of the Board have been asked to state in which schools they think it desirable pupil teachers should be apprenticed. In considering this matter it was felt, in view of the further requirements of the Board under the regulations for pupil teachers, that the work of the head teachers of the schools in which pupil teachers are apprenticed would be increased, and it is recommended that they be paid an honorarium for this extra work. After the forthcoming scholarship examination, the time table will be arranged in such a manner that pupil teachers will not be required to attend the classes on Saturday mornings.

LABOUR PARTY WANT TO FIGHT. Trades Council and the Liberal Overtures.

LIVELY DISCUSSION.

"The proposed Labour candidate has expressed himself favourable to fighting." So reported Mr. Oswald Buckley at the meeting of the Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday evening, and the prospect of a three-cornered contest gave rise to a lively , discussion. Mr. Buckley reported at some ' length upon the proceedings of the local Labour Representation Committee. At the meeting on June 21st, he said, the question of bringing for- ward a Labour candidate in Warrington was raised, but nothing had been definitely settled as yet. He could only state that the proposed candidate had expressed himself favourable to fighting, and that was as far as they had got. The committee were making preparations to hold a demonstration, and the pro- posed candidate would be invited to speak. The committee had decided to increase the executive by two members—Messrs. Podmore and Edward Edwardson. Another very important matter discussed was a letter received from the Liberal Executive asking them to meet a deputation from that body to consider the bringing out of a candidate at the next eleition. A. resolution, which had already appeared in the Press, was drawn up aad carried unanimously, stating their position as clearly as possible—that their rules were against thei?holding any conference ot negotiations of any description with either of the two political parties, that their object was the formation of a united Labour party, and that could not be done by their allying themselves with either of the parties. There were members paying to the committee who beloaged to the other political parties and had voted in the past for the party candidates and that being so it would not be wise for the committee, to take any step which might alienate members from them. Not a single word was "echoed" against the resolution and he was instructed to forward it to the Press and to Councillor Thmion, the Chairman of the Liberal Execu- tive. It was a rather unfortunate and regrettable inci- dent that just as the committee had made such a firm stand for independence, news came of the visit of Messrs. Shackleton and Henderson, M.P.'s, to Daven- port during the bye-election. They found the Liberal Press up and down the country—Wirrington included— congratulating those gentlemen upon their " common sense and broad-mindedness," and making a big shout about the fall of a" bold and bad independency. He could only say that all that sort of thing was very premature. There was no fall of the independency, and neither Messrs. Shackleton, Henderson, nor Crooks needed to be congratulated upon their broad-mindedness. They were broad-minded before. They made a stand in favour of independence and signed the constitution of the Labour Representation Committee, and they had not yet fallen away from it. Seeing that these "grossly erroneous statements ." appeared in the Press, he, as secretary of the committee, took it upon himself to get at the truth of the matter. He wrote to the National Labour Representation Committee Secretary and to the gentlemen concerned, and also to Mr. Hodge whose name was mentioned. He had received some very good replies, and those gentlemen assured him that they did not visit Davenport under Liberal auspices or attend any Liberal meeting. Mr. Henderson had for a long time previous to becoming an M.P., been a strong advocate of the United Kingdom Alliance, and his visit to Devenport was made in order to attend a meeting of that organization. The United Kingdom Alliance was a non-party and non-sectarian body. There was nodoubt that Hr. Henderson would never make any sug- gestion to the electors about supporting the Liberal candidate, bin he might probably have said that they had better give their votes to the man who was firsaes upon the temperance question. He did not think Mr. Henderson would be so unwise as to go any further than that. Mr. Hodge forwarded him a copy of a letter which he wrote to "The Clarion" in which he said "The statement appealing In your issue of the 24th inst. that I am "helping in the formation of a strong Liberal organization to further the candidature of Mr. Winston Churchill is absolutely untrue Whenever I find myself unable to act in harmony with the Labour Representation Committee constitution I shall resign. I will not sail under false colours." At a meeting of the Executive Committee of the National Labour Repre- sentation Committee, it was unanimously resolved That the Executive of the Labour Representation Committee having heard the explanation made by Messrs. Shackleton and Henderson, M.P.'s, as to their appearance in the constituencies of Market Harboro' ancljDevenport at the recent bye-elections; namely,that they did not appear under Liberal auspices nor attend any Liberal meeting, but went solely in the interests of the Labour movement, accepts the same,but in as much as such appearances during bye-elections afford oppor- tunities to the Press to misrepresent and thereby weaken the Labour Representation movement, declares that in future the Executive must decide what action if any should be taken at bye-elections." He (Mr. Buckley) could only add that he was surprised to hear that some of their members should have been so ready to believe the statements in the" Liberal Press as being" gospel truth," as they knew that such state- ments were made purposely to create dissension in the Labour movement. In future he would advise them to get at the real truth of the matter before relying on the statements in the Liberal Press. Councillor STOTT remarked that even if he had had any hesitation in refusing to meet the Liberal Executive previous to the meeting at which the matter was epn- sidered, he could not have had since. He had been repeatedly told by men who had been pleased to call themselves Conservatives, but who were now prepared to support an independent Labour candidate, that if the Labour Representation Corrraittee had thought fit to meet the Liberal Executive, they would have done their best to get their societies to withdraw from their affilia- tion. He thought the course the Labour Representation Committee had taken was the Wisest—(hear, hear)—as they had for some years been trying to wean men from the orthodox parties. If the committee were going to approach either party, he was fully convinced that the movement was doomed. It was only by sticking to the firm stand for independ- ence that their movement was going to be successful. The CHAIRMAN (Mr. J. Podmore, jun.,) entirely endorsed the action of the Labour Representation Com- mittee. As representing a certain trade, he could not possibly be a party to such a meeting. as was suggested. He for one, and he thought all the members of the Labour Representation Committee were determined that they would not prove false to what they had told their various branches—and that was that first and fore- most the committee must always be independent from the two political parties. Mr. SMITH thought it would have been wise for the committee to have met the Liberals. They had a great deal before them to consider in the shape of Mr. Chamberlain's scheme and other important matters, and titere was no doubt that both Liberals and Conservatives were tired of the present Government. He (Mr. Smith), had advocated that meeting for a compromise, and did so in order that there should not be the walk over for Mr.Pierpoint that a three-cornered contest would mean. They could take that statement as they liked, but he was sure'a three-cornered fight would result in a complete walk over, and in pressing it they were simply breaking the brickbone of the Labour Representation Committee in the Warrington district. (The Chairman "OhNo ") Well, that was his opinion. He had listened to every- body else, and now he was stating his views and the opinion of the majority of his trade. (" No, no.") The majority of his trade did not believe in a three-cornered fight because they were tired of the present Govern- ment and knew that such a contest would result in a walk over for the present member.

A VOICE: l'he;s3 may be a surprise packet. (Hear, hear, and." No "from Mr. Smith.) Mr. PODMORE : I believe there are two other dele- gates from Mr. Smith's society, and as he has stated that the majority of his trade are in favour of a com- promise I should like the other delegates to say whether that statement is correct.

The branch had nut had a meeting to take a vote on the question, and from his own experience in one of the largest mills, where two-thirds of the contributions came from, he would say distinctly that the statement was untrue as applied to that section. There were some in favour of the compromise, but Mr. Smith said the majority were, and he disputed that. (Hear, hear and applause.) Mr. SMITH (warmly) said that apart from disputing what tr. They wanted direct Laboun representation, and that was what they meant to hay He was one of those who believed they could win in a three-cornered fight, and if they were given the proper ' time he thought they would not only surprise Mr. Cros- field and his Liberal friends, but also the Tory candi- date. It had been said that their not coming to a com- promise would cause a split in the Labour party, but . that remained to be seen. (Hear, hear.) Such negotia- tions had taken place in other constituencies besides Warrington, and in not one instance had they proved satisfactory. Theyhad always ended in squabblings and misunderstandings. The Liberal party only asked for negotiations when they thought the Labour party was not strong enough to fight them. Directly they got the opinion that the Labour party was too strong for them, they were anxious to find excuses for standing down. ; Mr. SMITH: I hope they will be. Mr. BUCKLEY : We are going to show the Liberals and Conservatives that we are strong enough to be

Another DELEGATE said that apart from anything else he did not see why they should meet the Liberal Executive any more than the Conservatives. (Hear, hear). (Hear, hear.) _ _ _

CALL RIGHTS RESERVE!!

SOCIAL SERMNETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of " Search Lights on Land, Labour and Social Problems." THE EARL OF DERBY'S ESTATES.

"The motto, of the Stanley family is 'Sans changer' or without change, but it is diametrically opposed to the truth, for no family ever trimmed its sails more cleverly with the rising or falling winds, or to greater personal advantage."-_-HowA.RD Ev ANS.

The new Domesciay Book tells us that the Earl of Derby owns 63,869 acres of land, mostly in LAncashire and Cheshire, and that his rent roll, thirty years ago, amounted to £170,320 a year. When you have mastered these figures and grasped their significance, you will want to know how one family managed to acquire all these vast estates, and be able to dominate towns like Liverpool and Preston and Bury, and pocket more than three thousand pounds week as his toll on the plunder. a The Stauleys did not gain their wealth like the Cavendishes by begging monastic lands and trading in confiscated church property, and marrying a long suc- cession of rich heiresses. They did not even claim that their ancestors came over with the Conqueror, and were ennobled before they came. They begin their career as office-seekers, and servants to needy unscrupulous kings, and they developed into titled tax-eaters and profes- sional politicians. For more than one hundred years they were able to change successfully with every change of Government, and to put their money on the right horse and to be always on the winning side. But they came to grief when they backed King Charles 1., for they lost nearly all their. estates and money, and their leader.was beheaded. With the remnant of their fortune they bought some estates in Lancashire that have been as rich and profitable as gold mines. I.—THE FOUNDER OF THE WEALTH OF THE

===== 0002601_19040716_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
FAUILY WAS SIR JOHN STANLEY.

He was a shrewd, long-headed, far-seeing, country- squire, who owned a snaall estate at Newton-in-Maccles- field, in Cheshire, about five hundred years ago. He gained the favour and confidence of King Richard 11., who appointed him to the risky and thankless task of governing Ireland in his name, and the King gave him Castle Blake and some Crown land in that country to uphold the dignity of a king's deputy. In those -days of insurrection and revolution and civil war, the manorial lords had to walk very warily and circum- spectly, for they carried their lives in their hands, and if they took but one false step they stooda good chance of losing their estates and their honours and their heads into the bargain. , Richard 11. fell upon evil times and lost the confidence of his barons, and his rival, Henry of Bolingbroke, claimed his crown. Sir John Stanley was cute enough to read the signs of the times, and forsee what was corning, and though he owed a deep debt of gratitude to his friend and patron, King Richard, he thought he had better" be off with the old love and ;on with the new. So at the right moment, for his own interests, he dropped his allegiance to Richard and made his peace with Henry IV.

~.-ICrov7,-a-C-Cloidin-g to the ethics of the Stanleys this con- duct on the part of a public servant is not treason. . It is not even changing. It is consistency and continuity of service and worldly wisdom and common prudence, but it is a dangerous game after all, and if Richard had triumphed, Sir John Stanley would have been beheaded for his little games...

Henry W. found him a very handy, useful man. He sent him back to Ireland, and made him Lord Lieuten- ant of that distressful country. And when Civil War broke out once more in England he recalled him, and employed him in defeating Earl Percy and the northern nobles. For these services Sir John received the lord- ship of the Isle of 'llan, and 170,000 acres of land tuat had been taken from the rebellious barons.

When he had attained all this wealth -and dignity he prudently married Isabella, the daughter and heiress of Sir Thomas Latimm. This lady brought him gold and land in abundance, and a large manorial house in Liver- pool. With the king's consent he fortified this house and turned it into a castle, and made the name and fame of the Stanleys on the banks of the Mersey. THOMAS STANLEY lI.—HIS GRANDSON, SIR '

===== 0002601_19040716_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
BECAME THP; FIhST EARL OF DERBY.

He was a striking figure in the history'of this family, and he vastly increased its wealth, and fame, and political power. He was Lord Lieutenant of Ireland, and Lord Chamberlain under Henry VI., and like his grandfather he "never changed" but he had a knack of making all his treasons profitable and always appearing on the winning side. During the Wars of the Roses he invariably "held with the hare and ran with the hounds." He was a Lancastrian when that party happened to be in power. He was a Yorkist when that party had any plums or plunder to give away. When things were uncertain and doubtful, he was neutral, and he waited to see which way the cat jumped. He deserted Henry VI. to secure the favour of his rival Edward IV.. and somehow he got each new sovereign to believe in him and trust him. Richard 111. was one of those uni'(!rtunate kings who believed in his loyalty to him and his cause. He gave him a large pension and gralits of farms, and castles, and manors, and lordships, and made him steward of his household. And while he was in the'seryice and the pay of Richard he was secretly betraying his plans to his rival Henry Tudor. At the battle of Bosworth field, when the fate of dynasties hung in the balance, Sir Thomas Stanley and his brother William held their fighting men in reserve, and would not let them fight on either side till they saw who was going to win. When the tide of battle turned against Richard, and they saw his case was hope- less they turned against him too, and when the body of the king was found among the slain they put his crown on Henry's head and swore allegiance to him.

For these acts of treachery and disloyalty to the fallen king Henry created Sir Thomas Stanley, Earl of Derby, and conferred on him nearly all the forfeited estates that were taken from the defeated YorkistS. Baines in his History of Lancashire says, " Thus he acquired the estates of Sir Thomas Broughton,_ sir James Hirrington, Viscount Lovell, Sir Thomas Pilk- inpton, the Bythones, the Pootons, the Kirkbys, and at least twenty gentlemen's estates more." The next striking feature in the history of this family is Edward, the third Earl of Derby, who lived during the stormy times of the Reformation, and whu managed to keep his offices and pay all the time. Henry VIII. employed him to tight against the Catholics at the "Pilgrimage of Grace." Edward Vi. made him a commissioner for the advancement of the;, Reformation. Queen Mary made him Lord High Stennra of England, and employed him in sending Protestants to be burned at the stake. Queen Elizabeth chose lila for one of her Privy Councillors, and then he hunted the Catholics to death. He veered and changed like weather cock with every fresh political breeze that, came, and he made a pile of money out of them all, and yet he proudly boasted that he had never changed at all.

But there came an end to these land-grabbing, moseY,_ making, time-serving games. The men who grow rin. by other men's losses must expect to have losses 01 their own some day. 111. JAMES THE SEVENTH EARL OF DERBY-

===== 0002601_19040716_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
BROKE THE LUCK OF THE FAMILY.

He staked everything on the wrong horse, and lost and he brought ruin to himself, and terrible disasters to• his family. He lived in the days of Charles I, and. Cromwell, and when the Civil War broke out he was re- garded as an adherent of the Puritan party. But her afterwards joined the Royalists, and fought for them at the battle of Worcester. He also joined Prince Rupert in the attack on Bolton, in Lancashire, where a whole sale massacre took place, for which he, rightlY or wrongly, got the blame. He was arrested by the Parlia- mentarians, and tried on a charge of high treason snd he was beheaded in Bolton Market-place, October ,15th, 1652. ' This was the turning point in the history of the - family. The earl was dead, Lathom House was des; troyed, and Knowsley was little better than a heap er- ruins. The vast estates the Stanleys had acquired Nce,rie nearly all mortgaged, or sold, or confiscated to opholu the cause of Charles I. That Civil War was the 111 disastrous thing that ever came to the Darby family,an it nearly involved them in the loss of all things. After the Restoration, an attempt was made to get back the confiscated estates, and a Bill for their redenlP- tion was actually passed by both Houses of Parliament unanimously. But it was rejected, because Charles , IL would not approve of it, and he was universally blame because of the base ingratitude he had shown to the people who had sacrificed everything to save his o°l father. Then came another turn of fortune's wheel in the great industrial awakening in Lancashire. The StanleYs' atill possessed scattered estates in the ,county, but thei yielded little revenue to support an earldom till the' great boom in the cotton manufacture came. The steel° engine, and the spinning-jenny, and the power 1001 were invented, and factories were built all over Son/ Lancashire. Land went up in valle enormously, an ed the Stanleys reaped a rich har%%-3.,t. from this unearn increment. One of the best bargains ever made by mortal MO was the purchase of Moore's estate. 1710, by James, the, tenth Earl of Derby. It consisted of thousands of sel„ of freehold land in thirteen townships, but chieflY Bank Hall, Bootle, Kirkdale, and Liverpool, and the purchase money was only twelve thousand pounds. , „s If that land had to be bought now from the StaoLeY it would cost as many golden sovereigns as it cost bresst farthings then, and some of it could not be bought any price. In those days Liverpool was only a big fle.ts, ing village of about six thousand inhabitants, and, Le [rateable value was about 0£25,000. Most of ,the lanv bought consisted of sandhills and marshes and rabbit; warrens, and I have no doubt snme of his friends thong him a big fool for buying it at all. ted But Liverpool grew into a mightyseaport, and won- docks, and quays, and warehouses,and workmen's dWe" ings, and all these things wanted land to put them 011' so the Stanleys were able to charge what price theY pleased, as they had a monopoly of the available siteS- When Bootle Dock was made, Lord Stanley sold 5 portion of Moore's estate, consisting of 270,000 squares. yards of land. for 00,000, or nearly eight times e.s much as the whole estate cost at first. The joke of, e transaction was that nearly the whole of the land he sold for that dock was reclaimed from the sea at the expense of the Bootle ratepayers. S'xty years ago the ratepayers of Kirkdale %Vented strip of laid from the same estate on the river froatsg; and they gave Lord Derby .£17,500 for a narrow Piee 1,000 yards in length. The stem of short leases, with heavy fines renewals, has enormously increased the wealth of 02.7 Stanleys during the last fifty years, and when the Pry_ sent leases have to be renewed a great deal morf plunder will fall into their hands. Their land has bet° made valuable by the outlay and enterprise of the 005 mnnity, and they reap every penny of the unearA increment. When I hear toeople talking about what tine things tilt. Stanleys have done for Lancashire I invariably ask vithli Lancashire has done for the Stanleys? It has belpe_ them to recover from the disasters of the Civil Ws': and it has given them a goose that seems likely to gv on laying its golden eggs for ever.

MUNICIPAL BYE-ELECTION. TO THE ELEUTORS OF HOWLEY WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN. At the request of a large Durntner of ratepayers, I have consented to allow myself to be nominated as an iride- Pendent candidate at the bye-election in this ward. I have always been of opinion that it was a mistake to let politics interfere with municipal affairs. and I welcomed the advent of the Non-trading Association, if only to kill the compact between the political parties. If they have done so they deserve the thanks of the town, irrespective Of our opinions concerning their eneral policy, but personally,l think that they have justified their existence. They have scotched some ridiculous schemes, they have exposed serious irregularities in connection with a slack contract, and in many ways have enabled us to know more of the inner workings of the Town Council than formerly. As a large Ratepayer I have watched with iuterest the members of the Town Council embark upon the various costly public undertakings these last few years, and I consider that they have been guilty of lavish ex- penditure. The erection of new Police Buildings at a cost of abilut £3U,000 was a scandalous waste of public money, and if properly applied that sum was sufficient for that purpose and also the widening of IVarrington Bridge. The continued high rates and their probable increase is a serious matter to all concerned. They are detri- mental to the best interests of the town, a tax upon its trade and prosperity, and a cutting down of estimates as was recently done in Manchester would perhaps solve the question. lam ;not an advocate of false economy because I believe in efficiency, bu& when the rates are increasing by leaps and bounds it is time to economise some way. I am strongly opposed to increasing of salaries of already well paid officials of the Corporation by the ridiculous figure of £5O or £lOO at a time as was done recently, and respectfully suggest if any It creases are necessary that for a change the Council should commence at the other end of the social ladder. They have workman in their employ who are not over nivel. Some perform thankless duties for what is practically a bare living wage. I be- lieve in, and shall always support the payment of the standard rata of wages, both to workmen in the employ of the Corporation and also on contracts given by them. I am a strong supporter of the principle of Non-Trading. How can you expect purity or efficiency in municipal life if you allow members of the Council to use their positions in any way to gratify their personal gains The highest experts on municipal law have condemned the system. It is immoral ann leads to corruption and it should be declared illegal. In conclusion if you honouT me with yoer confidence, I shall do my utmost for the ward of which I am a resi- dent. I would always oppose extravagance and am confident that you would never reget electing me to the honourable position as one of the representatives of Howley Ward in the Town Council. I am, Ladies an: Gentlemen. Your obedient servant. THOS. CHANTLER, 92, Mersey Street; Warring ton.

I OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Latchford Baptist Church. There were large congregations at this church ea Sunday when the anniversary services were held. Mr, C. E. Parker, president of the Free Church Council, gave an address in the morning to the young people,and those who had the training of children. The preacher at the afternoon and evening services was the Rev. J. G. Greenough, M.A., of Dunton Bassett, near Lutter- worth ex-president of the Baptist Union and National Free Church Council. His afternoon text was taken from Romans xv. 13, and Mr. Greenough gave an in- teresting exposition of Paul's gospel of hope and of a message to the present age. In the evening the preacher took for his subject "Esau, that profane person." His sermon consisted of an analysis of the various forms of modern profanity and a plea for fidelity to conscience.. During the services well-rendered anthems were con- tributed by the chair, Mr. Peter Harrison being the organist. The free-will offerings for the day t amounted to £2O. Buckley-street Methodist Free Church. The Sunday school anniversary services were held at this church on Sunday. In the morning and afternoon, a large procession of Sunday scholars took place headed by the P.S.A. Brass Band under the leadership of Mr. A. Taylor. The morning service was conducted by Councillor Brough, who gave a discourse on " Antipas was my faithful martyr. In the afternoon, a musical service was rendered by members of tha Warrington Baptist and Buckley-street choirs (under theleadership of Mr. T. Brough) and the children's choir • (under the leadership of Mr. Albert Trow.) The service was interspersed with solos by Miss Eckersley, whose sweet sopfano voice was ' heard to great advantage in the solo, "Sun of my , soul," and M'r. S. Hassan effectively rendered " Come unto me." Mr. Atkinson was the accompanist. The', chair was occupied by Mr. Ben Few. In the evening ; the service was conducted by the Rev. H. Duunico, who, took for his text " In that day there shall be a fountain. opened to the. house of David,. and to the inhabitants of Jerusalem for sin and uncleanness." The choirs again rendered some pleasing anthems-during the evening set— vice, great praise being due to Mr. A. Trow for his careful training of the children. Miss M. Plinston, A.L.C.M., presided at the organ at all the services. The collections amounted to £5 15s. Bd. Heroes of Shipwreck and Social Wreck. The subject of the Rev. J. rouge's discourse in Wycliffe Congregational Church, on Sunday evening, was "Heroes of shipwreck, and heroes of social wreck." The text was: "Out of weakness were made strong: waxed valiant in fight." Dealing with the recant heroes of shipwreck, the terrible tale of the I sea unfolded at Grimsby was graphically described. Instances were given to show the " resplendent record" and the" magnificent displays of the heroines of the men of the sea " ; and the preacher observed that tin the last resort, when they had to choose between their own in- terests, welfare and actual safety, and those of others, let them say, "Others first." That N' yas the rule of the ways of God. It was the rule of the followers of Jesus Christ, the rule illustrated and formulated by the Great' Example. It would many times involve peril, risk, suffering, even possibly actual loss, but it led to honour and to immortality. The rev, gentleman then asked the congregation to transfer their thoughts to another wonderfully inspiring and instructive scene. It was not a scene of wreck, but, in more senses than one, a magni- ficent scene of salvation. The work of General Booth and the Salvation Army in reaching the masses was described, as well as the recent memorable spectacle in• which the General figured, a spectacle so telling and impres- sive that only one newspaper oP prominence had spoken of it in language of disparagement. The preacher added that there would he a great rush of loyal folk shortly %hen the King came to Liverpool to lay the stone of the new cathedral; it would no doubt be a scene of great interest and excitement, but he would sooner have seen the old General at the Crystal Palace in the midst of the thousands that had been converted to Christ by his soldiers, and many of them raised up from the deepest conceivable depths of social wreckage. Talk of heroes and heroism! Did ever the world see a truer heroism than that not only of the General himself, who wen out unhelped, unsupported to his great career, but also of the multitudes of his Salvation soldiers and lasses, who'had gone to the lowest haunts, to the vilest slums. and the worst of reprobates, down to the very scum of the social sink? They had "plucked brands from the burning," and rescued the drowning souls from the' deeps of sin and misery, and carried Satan's captives- triumphantly out of his very clutches. He (the speaker). had called attention to the matter, because it seemed• to him that Christians of every name, and churches of every order were challenged by General Booth and his army to a new an d finer consecration.

PASSIVE RESISTERS IN COURT. THREE CASES AT DARESBURY SESSIONS. _ Statements by the Revs. H. Dunnico and J. S. Hughes. --- The Court Room at Daresbury was on Tuesday the scene of passive resistance to the Education Act. Three defendants were summoned for the non-pay- ment of the education rate, namely, Rev. Herbert Dunnico, minister of the Baptist Church, G-olborne- street, Warrington, and living .in Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath; Rev. J. S. Hughes' 16, Bedford-street Stockton Heath, and minister of Hill Cliff° Baptist Church, and Mr. Henry Law,Whitefield-road, Stockton Heath. Mr. Law did not appear, but the Revs. H. Dunnico and J. S. Hughes succeeded in stating some of their objections to the Education Act, despite the attempt which was made to pin them down to legal objections. The magistrates on the bench were Mr. H. Neiid and Col. Ridgway. THE CASE AGAINST MR. DUNNICO. WHY HE REFUSES TO PAY THE RATE. The case against Mr. Dunnico was heard first. He was summoned for the non-payment of a portion of the rate amounting to 6s. 10d. the costs bringing the total amount to 9s. 10d'. Mr. Edwin Bradshaw, assistant overseer for Stockton Heath, said he applied for an order for payment of the amount due. The rates were made on the 22nd October and the sth May. They bad been duly allowed by the magistrates. He had duly demanded the payment of the rates. The Clerk (Mr. Hy. breenall) asked the defendant if he had any legal objection to take. Defendant: I beg to submit in the first place that the summons is not correct, because I am charged with refusing to pay the poor rate or any part thereof. I have already a receipt from the assistant overseer for the greater portion of the rate and in the second place I have not refused to pay it. The portion I have refused to pay is a rate demanded to support sectarian schools under private management. The clerk: That is not a legal objection. You know that perfectly well. Defendant: You have put me to some inconvenience ; in coming here. I claim the same right as other people ' to state very briefly my position. The Clerk: You are entitled to take a legal objec- tion to the payment of the rate. There is only one course for you to take. Defendant : I refuse to pay the rate for the reasons I have stated, namely, for the up-keep and teaching of doctrines with which I differ, and which I believe to 131 pernicious and detrimental to the highest welfare of the nation. I also refuse to pay a rate to aid in the carrying out of an Act which excludes Nonconformists from one half of the schocls in the country. Moreover the act is unconstitutional— Colonel Ridgway: I don't see that it is necessary to hear all that. The Clerk: You must take a legal objection. The Chairman: We are here to carry out the law however much we may have sympathy with you. Defendant: Even on legal grounio I could quote Dicey and Holt (Professor Dicey and Lord Holt) but I do not want to waste the time of the Bench. If I give a legal objection you will still be compelled to levy the rate. I hope, however, you will grant me the indulgence to state my position, because I am in the position of being able to put you to the inconvenience of taking my body, but I don' wish to de so. Colonel Ridgway: We understand what your object- tions are. You are a conscientious objector? Defendant : Yes, sir. Colonel Ridgway: From a legal point of view you have to pay the rate. We all have to do the same. The Clerk (to the Bench) : You simply make the order. Colonel Ridgway: This is only a waste of the Court's time, and your own time. The Clerk (after turning to the Bench) : The order is made. • Defendant: I thank you for your courtesy. I refuse to pay. I have stated my objections. Colonel Ridgway: I quite understand you. • THR CASE OF MR. LAW. The next case was against Mr. Henry Law, Whitefield road, Stockton Heath. The Rev. J. S. Hughes: Mr. Law is sorry he cannot be here to answer for himself. I have promised to appear for him. The Clerk stated that in this case the amount due was 9s. Bd. Mr. Bradshaw gave evidence as to the levying of the rate, ecc. A similar order was made as in the last case. MR. HUGHES ASKS FOR FOUR MINUTES. The Rev. J. Samuel Hughes, 16, Bedford-street, Stockton Heath, and minister of Hill Cliff° Baptist Church, was summoned in respect of Bs. 10d. balance of rate and costs due. The Clerk: Do you admit that? Defendant: Yes, sir. I admit that. Mr. Bradshaw gave evidence proving the debt. The Clerk (to defendant) : Have you any legal ob- jection to take to this rate? Defendant : I have no legal objection, but there are some things that may be legally right that are morally wrong. I have been a ratepayer inithe district for a considerable number of years, rand it is exceedingly grievous to me to have to appear in court in answer to a matter like this. And the circumstances being ex- ceptional. I think that men in our position ought to have some special reason for the position we are taking up. We have that special reason, and if you will per- mit me four minutes by the clock I will tell you those reasons. The Clerk: Oh, no, sir! Defendant: There will be more time wasted in in- terruptions if I am not free from them. The Clerk : There will be no time wasted in inter- ruptions because you have to conform to a court of justice. Defendant: I take exception to the statement on my summons. The Clerk : That won't do. Defendant: The summons states that I have not paid the poor rate or any part thereof. It is absolutely untrue. I have never refused to pay the poor rate. have paid so far as I am aware every penny of it. Colonel Ridgway: I think you might say you are a conscientious objector ? Defendant : I am. Colonel Ridgway: That is enough. Defendant: I think a person in my position ought to be allowed to give exceptional reasons for the course he is taking. The Clerk: No,Mr. Hughes, it is not so. You are in a court of justice and a court of law, and the only thing you cart do is to raise a question of law. You admit the legality of the rate made and therefore your objection is not a legal one because the rate has been duly made and allowed by the magistrates. Colonel Ridgway: I think you have ; ained all you want. You want to appear as a conscientious objector. If you want to say anything else you can hire the Parr Hall, or go into some countryplace, and have a meeting but while in a court of law you must conform to the law. The Clerk: In a court of law you must have a legal objection. • Defendant: I thoroughly understand that, but at the same time, you allow other witnesses, as we have seen here this morning, to travel considerably from the point and I shall not take more than three minutes of your time if free from interruption. The Clerk: You are not entitled to take up the time of the court. Defendant: I can only thank you. Colonel Ridgway : We quite understand your posi tion. Defendant : I object on religious grounds, on the unfairness of the Act-- Colonel Ridgway: Excuse me. Hold a public meet- ing in some public place. Defendant : All right we will have a public meeting. The order for payment of the rate was then made. AN APPLI CAT/ON. A few minutes later Mr. Hughes, addressing the magistrates, said: I appls to have the orders put to- gether on one form. The Clerk: No, I advise the Bench not to do that. Defendant: It is done in some courts. ' The Clerk : Some courts have made one rule, some another. The Assistant Overseer said the overseers had no ob- jection. The Clerk : That alters the matter. Very well then. - • Defendant : We have no wish to be obstreperous. The application was granted.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. __— The fortnightly meeting of this authority was held at, Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. T. Baxter presid- ing. There were also present :—Messrs. J. Douglas, W. M. Morris, and J. W. Temperley (representatives of Runcorn ,the members of the Rural District Council,and Messrs G. F. Ashton (clerk),W.E. Hough(deputy clerk), J. Cook,W. Riley and W. R.de Prez (relieving officers), and J. Cunuah (workhouse master). THE CLERK GOING TO CAMP. The Clerk was granted leave of absence from the next Board meeting as he will be at camp with the Runcorn Volunteers. COMBINATION AND CLASSIFICATION QUESTIONS. In forwarding a resolution passed by the sub-com- mittee appointed by the General Conference Committee of the North-Western Poor Law Conference District, Mr. George Macdonald wrote that the suggestions were that the Lancashire Asylums Board should arrange for the erection of buildings of a plain and inexpensive char- acter for the accommodation of harmless chronic lunatics and imbeciles chargeable to the rates of the County of Lancaster, and that the Cheshire County Council should be approached on the provision of separate asylum accommodation for paupers of the same class charge- able to the Cheshire Unions. The Lancashire Asylums Board had agreed to the proposals, and had decided to submit to the Lunacy Commissioners a scheme for separate asylum accommodation for chronic, harmless insane patients and imbeciles in less expensive buildings than the existing asylums. The resolution asked the Cheshire County Council to follow the example of the Lancashire County Council. THE CHAPLAIN'S HOLIDAYS. The Rev. L. D. Lyon Bennett, chaplain to the Work- house, wrote that he was leaving home for a change, and would not return until August sth. In the mean- time the Sunday services would be taken by the Rev. Joseph Llewellyn, assistant curate of Kirkbey, near Liverpool. ADMIRAL JONES-PARRY AND THE MEDICAL OFFICERS Admiral Jones-Parry gave notice that at the next meeting he would move that when vacancies aruse in the list of medical officers the appointments be subject to annual confirmation ; also that the salary of the medical officer for the Budworth District be reduced from £lOO to £BO. THE COST OF SCHOOL BUILDINGS. Mr. Collier gave notice that at the next meeting he would move that the County Council be requested to reconsider the proportion payable by them towards the cost of building provided schools, and that in fixing the amount they should take into consideration the current rate and ratable value of the parish in which such schools were to be erected. FINANCIAL-THE VACCINATION FEES. Mr. Woodward presented the financial statement which showed that the payments included £92 3s. 9d. for vaccination fees. As compared with the June quarter of last year the amount was a reduction of about £3l. It was ,410 less than the payment for the March quarter of this year. The charge for the main- tenance of lunatics in the County Asylum during the quarter was £3BB 3s. 11d, as compared with £398 Bs. lid. in March. There were cheques for £26 for the main- tenance of children in Pantasaph Orphanage and £7 16s. for the maintenance of two boys at Buckley Hall, Rochdale. The general statement.of accounts showed a balance in hand of £lBl 3s. 2d. The report was adopted. DR. MCDOUGAL'S LEAVE OF ABSENCE. The Clerk was instructed to write to Dr. McDougal asking him in future to inform the Board who would discharge his duties in the event of his absence from Runcorn at any tiine. It was stated that the other medical officers had always made a practice of furnishing this information when they took holidays. • COLONEL WALKER'S VOTE IN PARLIAMENT. In the House of Commons on Monday, during the discussion on the Licensing Bill, Mr. Lloyd George' moved to disallow the vote of the hon. member for Widnes (Colonel Hall Walker) on the ground that the hon. member had a direct personal pecuniary interest in the vote. The amendment related to the distribution of a fund to be raised for compensation for the non- renewal of Licences. By the amendment, the owner of licensed premises would have his compensation money reduced by the amount of the servants' wages for three months, and the compensation given to the licencee. The hon. member for Widnes would be directly affected by the passing of the amend- ment. On 30th April, 1901, on a motion to disallow the vote of Mr. Macartney, who was a director of the London and North-Western Railway Company, the Speaker said it was for the House itself to decide I whether a member voting had a direct pecuniary interest or not. In 1896, a committee sat to consider this ques- tion of members' votes, and Mr. Speaker Peel said, "It was for the House to decide in each individual case before it, whether a member has a direct interest in the vote given." (Opposition cheers.) The present was distinctly a case whicit ought to be left to the House to I decide. (Renewed Opposition cheers.) He moved that the vote be disallowed.—The Chairman declined to accept the motion, stating that a member was entitled to vote on a question of a public and general nature, which incidentally involvedi,the pecuniary interest of a class, including the member.

If every person were aware of a perfect remedy for such wounds and would apply it, then such reports would cease. The remedy, states an American contemporary, is simple, always at hand, can be applied by anyone, and, what is better, is infallible. It is simply to smoke the wound or any wound that is bruised or inflamed with a woollen cloth. Twenty minutes in the smoke will take the pain out of the worst case of inflammation arising from such a wound. People may sneer at this remedy as much as they please, but when they are afflicted with such wounds let them try it. It is a simple "aseptic "or germ-killer.
